I should say a few words about how this model works. It is comparing the long-term outcome of buying a home to the counterfactual outcome of renting. In the first year of the model, the owner buys a home. They pay a down payment and closing costs. The renter instead invests the down payment and closing costs in a portfolio of stocks and then pays rent which increases 1% per year above inflation in the model. The total cash flow costs of owning a home, including property taxes, maintenance costs, and mortgage payments are assumed to be the total cash flows available to both the renter and owner. If rent costs less than the owner's cash flow costs, the renter is saving and investing the difference into a portfolio of stocks. If rent costs more than the cash flow costs of owning, a common scenario with the mortgage paid off, the renter funds the cash flow shortfall from their portfolio. This scenario assumes that the renter is investing in their RRSP, TFSA, and FHSA, which are not taxable. But I will come back to the implications of taxable investing later. To come back to the main points on home ownership not being such a great investment, salience bias leads people to focus on what they bought their home for and what they sold for, ignoring the ongoing costs of living in the home and especially at long horizons, overestimating the compound returns on their home. Properly accounted for, the returns to housing are typically not special and similar financial outcomes can be achieved by renting and investing in stocks. There are multiple academic studies based on Canadian and US data showing this to be true. Let's move on to the value of mortgage debt. I'll come back to forced savings and tax treatment later. Mortgage financing is one of the most favorable forms of financing available to most people. It is generally low cost and does not get called when the home value drops like a margin loan for stocks can. Borrowing early in life to get exposure to assets is arguably a good thing and mortgage debt is a great way to do that. It's also a double-edged sword. Many people in Canada were squeezed financially when mortgage rates increased after the pandemic and they can be a burden if your property value declines and you want to move. The main point I want to make here though is that a mortgage is not a silver bullet for owners to build more wealth than renters. Take an example where a home is bought in cash by a homeowner. The counterfactual renter invests the cost of buying the home, in this case the full price of the home plus closing costs in stocks and then pays rent. The owner doesn't have a mortgage, but they do still have some costs like maintenance costs and property tax, but their cash flow costs are much lower than the renters, meaning the renter in the model has to draw from their portfolio to cover the difference. The thing is, the expected returns on the renters's investment portfolio are more than enough to make up for the difference. The owner is paying a large implicit cost by having so much home equity relative to the renters's large stock portfolio. After 50 years, the renters's wealth exceeds the owners by $2.2 $2 million. Next, consider the same case, but instead of paying in cash, the owner puts down a 20% down payment and finances the remainder of the purchase with a mortgage. Now, the owner has large cash flow costs because in addition to property taxes and maintenance, they have a mortgage payment. The renter initially invests the smaller down payment into a stock portfolio and then saves the monthly difference between the owner's higher ongoing cash flow costs and their rent into an investment portfolio. The net worth difference after 50 years in this case is $467,000. Financing with a mortgage was more financially advantageous for the owner than paying cash, but the renter was still able to come out ahead without borrowing any money. The owner's relatively high cash flow costs allowed the renter to save more into their higher expected return portfolio, all else equal. The next misunderstanding I want to cover is that people who own homes are wealthy because they own homes. It is true that the average net worth of a homeowner is significantly higher than that of a renter. Both in Canada and the United States, but this is not because owning a home is an automatic path to wealth. Renters tend to be younger, have lower incomes, and spend more of their incomes on housing costs, making building wealth by any means more of a challenge. Buying a home would not solve these problems. And some research shows that home ownership could actually negatively impact the happiness of low-income households due to the financial burden of home ownership. A 2017 paper, a revision of the American dream of home ownership, seeks to answer the question of why owner households tend to be wealthier than renters. The authors find that it can be attributed to choices within the scope of the individual, like how much the renter saves, rather than through the impact of exogenous market variables like housing or stock market returns. They showed that households that failed to reinvest the cash flow difference between renting and owning accumulate less wealth. And importantly, they find that property appreciation plays only a minor role in the results for homeowner and renter wealth differentials. This analysis shows, as I have already said, that renting and owning can theoretically have similar financial outcomes, but this requires renters to save and invest the cash flow cost difference between renting and owning. In some cases, this is simply not possible due to limited financial resources, which is why a lot of people rent. A problem owning a home rather than renting would not solve. Whether renters with sufficient cash flow available to save will actually be disciplined savers is a valid concern, and I will come back to that later. Next is one of the most misunderstood aspects of home ownership, that a paid for home reduces your housing costs. This seems like it has to be right on the surface because once your mortgage payment is gone, your cash flow costs are much lower. You still have some ongoing costs like property taxes and maintenance, but mortgage payments tend to be the largest cash flow cost for owners, making owning feel cheaper than renting once the mortgage is gone. The problem here is mental accounting bias. Separating cash flow and capital into separate mental accounts. When both capital and cash flow are considered, the reality is that paying off a mortgage actually increases housing costs. Because for most people, the opportunity cost of having home equity rather than other investments exceeds the cost of borrowing money from the bank. We saw this play out in the earlier example of paying cash for a home. To be clear, there are lots of benefits to having a paid for home. It reduces risk, reduces cash flow costs, which do matter for people who are cash flow constrained, and it feels great. But a paidoff home is counterintuitively more expensive to live in than a mortgage financed home due to the large opportunity cost of home equity. The opportunity cost is smaller or even negative in some cases for people who would alternatively be invested in very conservative assets like guaranteed investment certificates or cash. The last misconception I want to cover is that homeowners are happier than renters. I'm not going to go through all of them here, but in another video I reviewed multiple academic papers showing that owning a home is unlikely to increase your life satisfaction. And if it does, it probably won't increase it as much as you expect. In some cases, owning a home may even decrease happiness by soaking up your active leisure time with home maintenance tasks and saddling you with a big pile of debt. As a former renter and current homeowner, I can tell you that the psychological weight of owning a home rather than renting is significant. I think it's important for people to understand those misconceptions, but there are definitely distinct benefits to owning a home. In Canada, increases in the value of your primary residence are not taxed. We do have tax sheltered investment accounts that allow investments like stocks and bonds to grow tax-free, but their contribution room is limited. For someone who has filled up all of their registered savings accounts and is investing their savings in a taxable account, the opportunity cost of home equity is a lot lower because the after tax rate of return on counterfactual investments is lower. Revisiting the example from earlier that showed the renter coming out ahead in the long run, making their investments taxable puts them at a disadvantage relative to the owner. And that disadvantage gets larger with an increasing tax rate. An important note here is that this does not mean that owning is always better than renting for taxable investors with high incomes. But it does mean that the equilibrium rent, the rent that makes renting and owning financially comparable, is lower. Instead of $2,119, this renter would need to find a place for $1,700 to have a financially comparable outcome to an owner. This is a good argument for buying a home in some cases. taxable investors with high incomes who want large and more expensive homes may be better off owning. Probably the strongest argument for owning is that owned homes, especially without a mortgage, hedge the costs of living in a specific home. A hedge means that it increases in value when housing costs increase and decreases in value when they decrease. This is very useful to you if you want to stay in a specific home for a long time. Rising housing costs in an area could result in renters being priced out and having to move and being unable to afford buying a home in that area. Owners are protected because they already own the home. Rising housing costs and home values don't really affect them. They're hedged. To be clear, a hedge can come both ways. More recently in Canada, we have seen falling rents and home prices. An owner who never wants to move is unaffected by falling prices, but an owner who decides to relocate may have to absorb a loss in the value of their property. The other thing to keep in mind is that it can be hard to find certain types of rentals. I bought a house in the forest during the pandemic after renting for my whole adult life because I could not find a rental in the forest. Finally, possibly one of the biggest benefits of owning a home is behavioral. A mortgage on an owned home is a powerful commitment device. It forces savings, and a home is a lot harder to panic sell in a down market than a portfolio of stocks. When I model long-term outcomes for renters and owners, I typically assume that the renter is saving all of the difference in cash flow costs between renting and owning. If they're not doing that, the financial comparability of renting and owning falls apart quickly. This means that financially successful renting requires extreme discipline to match the wealth of owning. Most people won't skip a mortgage payment, but they might defer contributing to their RRSP. Discipline extends to investing, too. It is well known that investors tend to trail the returns of the assets they invest in due to behavioral errors like buying high and selling low. These points are extremely important and honestly they make me think that most people should probably buy a home if they can afford to. To be clear, the nerds watching my channel are not most people. Disciplined and informed renters can have good financial outcomes. Many of the common arguments in favor of home ownership are wrong. Homes are not great investments. Home ownership is not the causal factor in making people wealthy. Wealthier people can afford to own homes. Mortgages are not a wealth hack. Having a mortgage-free home does not reduce your housing costs, and homeowners are not happier than renters. This does not mean that renting a home is superior to owning, but it does mean that renting deserves more credit than most people are willing to give it. That said, there are cases where owning a home makes sense. favorable tax treatment, the ability to hed the cost of living in a specific home, the lack of availability of certain types of housing for rent, and behavioral benefits can all be important in the long run. A disciplined renter who matches their rental cost to the total cost of owning a home, saves the cash flow cost difference between renting and owning into higher expected return investments like stocks, and doesn't make costly behavioral errors with their investments, can expect to accumulate comparable wealth to a homeowner. Thanks for watching. I'm Ben Felix, chief investment officer at PWL Capital. Tell me what you think people get wrong about the rent versus own decision in the comments.

The fact that index funds do need to trade breaks the basic principle of the arithmetic of active management, at least as it's commonly understood. It doesn't shatter it. Index fund implementation costs come in three main forms. Adverse selection, buying stocks when firms, the underlying businesses issuing stock, wants to sell, and selling when firms want to buy. This adverse selection can happen when firms move between the private and public markets and when existing public firms issue and buy back shares. The authors show in the paper as we should expect that index funds mechanically respond to these changes in market composition and as a result they incur adverse selection costs. It's pretty simple when you think about it. Firms companies that list on the stock market are generally pretty savvy and they act in their own best interests. For example, firms will issue stock when they think their share price is high and they will buy back stock when they think their share price is low. Indexes quickly incorporate these changes to market composition. Buying stock when firms issue and selling when they buy back and index funds follow suit. That makes perfect sense if the objective of the index is to represent the market. But it puts index fund rebalancing exactly at odds with firms own beliefs about their share prices. It's a similar situation for IPOs, which tend to occur in waves, often coinciding with high market valuations and to underperform in the long run. I've got a video on IPOs if you want to learn more about that. The fact that the alpha the portion of returns unexplained by factor exposures is negative and statistically significant suggests that there are adverse selection costs beyond the factor loadex. The long short portfolio that buys index additions and IPOs and sells index deletions and delistings has an annual return of negative 5.5% with a statistically insignificant alpha. These findings are consistent with observations from other research suggesting that IPOs and seasoned equity offerings have poor returns in general, that firms issue stock to meet index fund buying demand when stock valuations are high, and that the mechanical index fund buying of stocks after IPOs leads to underperformance. Figuring out how much this is actually costing index funds is not easy to do because the same trades affecting the index fund are affecting the benchmark index. Benchmarking an index fund against its underlying index will make it look like everything is fine. The authors compare these counterfactual delayed rebalancing portfolios to a quarterly calendar-based rebalancing portfolio, which resembles how live index funds actually implement. But if it's costing another 0.32% to own relative to a more flexible rebalancing strategy, it's actually kind of expensive. That's it. That's adverse selection. The second potential issue with mechanical rebalancing is price pressure on index reconstitution. This is distinct from adverse selection, but has historically had estimated costs of a similar magnitude. When an index changes to include or exclude a stock, those changes are announced before index funds buy or sell the stock. This effect increases or decreases the price of the stock in anticipation of it being bought or sold in the future. But that effect is not permanent. The result is that index funds may have a drag on returns stemming from their predictable buying and selling behavior. Similar to adverse selection, this cost is not easily visible because it affects indexes and index funds equally. Research on this idea goes back to the 1980s with two highly cited 1986 papers where the authors document that when stocks get added to the S&P 500, their price goes up and that these increases are likely due to anticipation of index funds buying the stocks in the future. The problem is that the price effect reverses after the stocks have been bought, resulting in index funds buying at temporarily high prices. Again, index funds are buying high, selling low based on short-term price pressure, and then eating the reversal when the price pressure is gone. Another interesting point that is less frequently discussed is the valuations of stocks that get added to and removed from indexes. This has an implicit cost for index funds. Looking at longer horizons, the 2023 paper, earning alpha by avoiding the index rebalancing crowd shows that in the year before S&P 500 index inclusion, stocks tend to have very high returns and in the year after they tend to have very low returns. The opposite is true for index deletions. Over such a long time frame, this can't all be the index inclusion effect. Instead, the author showed that additions are 92% more expensive based on multiple valuation ratios than the market and discretionary deletions are 55% cheaper than the market at the times of their respective additions and deletions. This total effect has declined in the second half of the sample, but from 2005 to 2021, the total one-year difference in returns between index additions and deletions is still a statistically significant 14.8% in favor of deletions. This is likely a combination of the index inclusion effect and a mean reversion related to valuations. The authors show that alternative index fund implementations, including delaying index rebalancing by 12 months after a change in the index, can add up to 0.23% to index fund returns. Both the index inclusion effect and the buy high sell low effect would be less pronounced for a total market fund like VTI than an S&P 500 fund like VO. But the adverse selection cost discussed earlier still applies to VTI from multiple angles. Adverse selection, the index inclusion effect, and buying high and selling low. Index funds are not as efficient as they could be. To bring this back to practical reality, Dimensional Fund Advisors, a fund company that I reviewed in detail in a recent video, is known for funds that tilt towards certain types of stocks like small caps, value stocks, and stocks with robust profitability in an effort to beat the market. But the beginning of the end of the world as we know it due to insert crisis here gets people out of their seats and moves asset prices. To get through market crashes, investors need to understand a few things. They need to understand the historical resilience of stock markets going back hundreds of years. As far back as we have data, the optimists, those who have invested in stocks, have typically triumphed. Global stock returns have been meaningfully positive for at least 125 years, delivering a little more than an annualized 5% real return that is adjusted for inflation measured in US dollars from 1900 through 2024. That is a long history of positive real returns over a period where a lot of scary stuff has happened. That's why stocks are great long-term investments for people who are able to take risk. In Robert Schiller's 2017 paper, Narrative Economics, he explains, "Narratives are human constructs that are mixtures of fact and emotion and human interest and other extraneous details that form an impression on the human mind." Narratives can be extremely powerful. They can change how we interpret facts and cause us to ignore base rate probabilities while drawing parallels between our current situation and familiar past narratives. To be clear, the narrative does not change the facts of the situation, but it does change how people respond to the facts. Robert Schiller, of course, is the Yale Economist who won the 2013 Nobel Memorial Prize in Economic Sciences for his work on bubbles in asset prices. Schiller defines a bubble as a social epidemic that involves extravagant expectations for the future. Bubbles are typically associated with high asset prices, but they go both ways. In the paper, Schiller explains the narratives that may have played a role in some of the biggest economic events of modern history. Take for example the 1920 to 21 US recession and stock market decline. World War I had ended 14 months prior. The effects of the 1918 influenza pandemic were still lingering. The US had been dealing with race riots and a public fear of communism was growing. Combined, these were the basis for an unsettling narrative of economic uncertainty. Put yourself in the shoes of someone living through that. It wasn't just a market crash. People were losing faith in the entirety of the US economic system. It wasn't that long ago that we all lived through a pandemic that shut down the global economy and again made people around the world question whether things would ever go back to the old normal. Yet, here we are. Today, we're living through a global trade war. Most economists agree that tariffs are not good for the economy, and the stock market has clearly expressed its agreement. Tariffs reduce expected earnings for companies, and trade policy uncertainty increases risk. I don't want to comment further on the narratives in this case because I don't want the comments to blow up with political arguments, but we all know why this time feels different. To be clear, I'm not discounting the possibility that this time is different. But every time is different in its own way. That's the point. That's why crashes happen. Global financial markets have seen periods of globalization followed by contraction before. In the early 1900s, there were active stock and bond exchanges in at least 40 countries around the world. And European investors, just like investors today, were being urged by the experts to internationally diversify their portfolios. That was a golden age for investing, similar to what we've experienced in recent decades. Everything changed starting with World War I. Global finance contracted, protectionism and tariffs increased and communism and fascism saw a rise. Some stock markets closed temporarily and some closed permanently. It was a bad time to be an investor. Will it get that bad again? I don't know. I do know that despite the rough years following the contraction of global finance in the 1900s and including the market closures and total losses in Russia and China, global markets have been resilient, delivering positive real long-term returns to investors. The thing is while every crisis is unique, every time is different. Scary narratives and market crashes and subsequent recoveries are as old as financial markets themselves. The phenomenon of markets bouncing back after crashes was studied in the 2017 paper negative bubbles. What happens after a crash? The authors study crashes in 101 global stock markets from 1692 through to 2015. They identify 1,032 events where a market declined by more than 50% over a 12-month period. They find that these extremely large annual stock market declines are typically followed by positive returns. This large empirical data set is immune to any narratives when we step back and observe it. Each of those 132 crashes was probably accompanied by intimidating reasons to expect worse things to come, similar to what people might be feeling today. But we have the ability now to step back and observe the outcomes of these past events free from the accompanying narratives to establish an understanding of the base rates of what is reasonable to expect after a market crash based on what has happened in the past. A related point is that when stock prices do decline, expected stock returns tend to increase. From the perspective of long-term financial planning, the increase in expected returns offsets a lot of the effects of the price decline. For investors still contributing to their portfolios, this could even be seen as good news. That should make people feel better. But when crashes do happen, investors subjective expectations of stock market returns tend to become more pessimistic rather than more optimistic. Again, a case of narratives dominating base rates. None of this means that you should do nothing. Market crashes present rare opportunities to learn about your risk tolerance. One of the often underappreciated elements of a risk profile is risk composure. how you have actually felt during past market crashes. It's worth checking in with yourself. How do you feel? How are you reacting? Are you losing sleep? If things feel worse than you expected they would when you chose your asset allocation, it might be a sign that your portfolio is too aggressive. On the other hand, if you're in a conservative portfolio, have a long time horizon, and are psychologically sailing through the current situation, it might be a sign that you can take more risk. This is also a good time to revisit your financial plan. Financial plans should be built to withstand the effects of short-term market volatility, but if a change is warranted, it's better to know about it sooner rather than later. Market crashes are objectively not fun. Narratives are a powerful component of them, and they can affect the way we interpret facts. Rather than focusing on the specifics of any one crisis which will always be unique, I think it's important to step back and look at the broad set of data of which we are fortunate to have hundreds of years before responding to an event like a meaningful decline in stock prices. Regardless of the narrative, you should be invested in an asset allocation that you can stick with in both good times and bad. Getting out when things get bad is one of the best ways to sabotage your returns. If living through a stock market drop has been too painful, it might be a good time to ask yourself if you were taking too much risk to begin with. I am not saying that everything will be okay by next week. The market might keep dropping. After each subsequent drop, the decision not to sell after the previous drop might seem regrettable. Everything is obvious in hindsight. Getting out of the market might feel right today, but the second decision of getting back in is a lot harder to get right, and getting it wrong can be extremely expensive. It is true that the economy might take a tariff related hit, but this is why you have an emergency fund. Stocks might continue to drop, but this is why you invested in a risk appropriate portfolio. In the moment when things are uncertain, it can be challenging to make good long-term decisions. This is why you made a plan in calmer times, and now is the most important time to stick to it. Thanks for watching. I'm Ben Felix, chief investment officer at PWL Capital. I hope you're all doing okay. Tell me how you're feeling in the comments.

Let's get into it. Status quo life cycle investment advice, which means investment advice that spans the stages of life from early savings through to estate planning, is a portfolio that starts off in stocks and then gradually increases its weight in bonds over the life cycle, generally resulting in an aggressive equityheavy portfolio early in life and a more conservative bond heavy portfolio in retirement. This advice is present in academic literature, practitioner textbooks, and even embedded in financial products like target date funds with trillions of dollars invested in them. Rather than relying on the same kinds of basic assumptions about expected returns that support status quo life cycle asset allocation, this paper uses a method called block bootstrap to simulate possible return scenarios for stocks, bonds, and bills based on historical data for 39 developed countries with data going as far back as 1890. This gives more than 2,600 years of countrymon return data to draw from. The data being examined include market capitalization weighted stock market indexes, government bonds, and government bills. That detail matters because changing the inputs by adding in corporate bonds, which total market bond index funds tend to contain, or tilts in the stock portfolio toward things like small cap value stocks, could change the results. The results I'm going to talk about are specific to the assets being tested. Next, I'm going to explain how the block bootstrap methodology works and why it's used for the analysis. I told you this would be nerdy. block bootstrap randomly grabs a chunk of data with an average length of 10 years from the large sample of historical returns and places it in the simulated series. It then grabs another chunk with an average length of 10 years and so on until the bootstrap data series is long enough to fill the hypothetical investor's life cycle. Block bootstrap maintains important characteristics of stock and bond returns that don't show up in a normal distribution of independent and identically distributed returns. Assumptions often used in modeling asset allocation decisions. For example, long-term stock returns tend to be serially correlated, meaning that what happened yesterday affects what happens tomorrow. Extreme negative returns tend to be followed by positive returns. Periods of high volatility tend to be clustered together, and returns tend to be skewed, meaning that they do not follow a normal distribution. Bonds tend to exhibit mean aversion, where bad returns are often followed by more bad returns, and domestic stock and bond returns are increasingly correlated at longer horizons. The authors of this paper take the view that the historical experiences of developed countries are interchangeable for the purpose of thinking about the expected distribution of returns for any given country. For example, the historical experiences of Australia, Japan or Italy are relevant for thinking about the expected future experience for Canada. Domestic stocks in this setting does not refer to any specific country but to the possible experience of a domestic investor in any developed country based on the combined experience of all countries in the sample. As an example, one domestic bootstrap draw might pull eight years of returns from Canada, 14 from Australia, 10 from Japan, 15 from New Zealand, 13 from Finland, and 10 from Germany. All domestic data are real returns for a given country measured in that country's currency. They're then strung together into, in the case of this example, 70 years of data representing one possible return experience that a domestic stock investor in a developed country could expect based on what has happened in other developed countries throughout history. For each draw of domestic returns, international returns are the market cap weighted portfolio of all countries excluding the domestic country measured in the domestic country's currency. Bonds and bills are also matched to the domestic country for each draw. The optimal levered portfolio uses 55% leverage on an all equity portfolio and materially decreases utility matched savings relative to the unlevered base case. Using the same amount of leverage to invest in a portfolio of 60% domestic stocks and 40% bonds would require a savings rate of 19.1% to match the utility of the base case unlevered 100% equity portfolio. In the lowest cost of leverage case, which keep in mind likely requires using derivatives, the household borrows 100% of their wealth and invests in a portfolio of 28% domestic stocks, 57% international stocks, and 15% bonds. Bonds finally make an appearance in the optimal levered portfolio. There are some investment products that have built-in lowcost leverage using derivatives and offer access to a levered portfolio of stocks and bonds. In those cases, the fee charged on the product would need to be included in estimating the cost of leverage. The average expense ratio on a leveraged ETF is 1.05%, much higher than most total market index funds. The 2025 paper, Beyond the Status Quo, a critical assessment of life cycle investment advice, is a thoughtful analysis of life cycle investment advice using an innovative method to simulate the expected distributions of stock, bond, and bill returns from the actual historical experience of investors in developed countries around the world. as far back as 1890. Capturing the characteristics of long-term returns and using global data produces results that challenge the conventional wisdom on life cycle asset allocation. I would not take the precise results from this paper as instructions for what to do with your portfolio, but I do think that they are directionally useful. Stocks look better at long horizons than at short horizons. Nominal bonds look worse, and the correlation between domestic stocks and bonds increases at long horizons. All making bonds less attractive for long-term investors. Even the paper's critics agree on this directionally, but disagree on the magnitude and interpretation of the result. International diversification is very important. Again, critics agree here, too. And a little bit of home country bias for investors outside the US is probably not a bad thing. Timing asset class exposures based on valuations has little effect on long-term outcomes. Even if you know you're living in a high valuation quintile, a bit of leverage can look pretty good if you can access it at a low cost and stick with it for the long term. But the basic finance theory suggesting that levering up portfolios of stocks and bonds is universally superior to investing 100% in stocks falls apart at long horizons. It's also important for me to reiterate that this analysis is based on simulations derived from historical data and that the future could look different from history. Expected stock returns could be lower in the future than the past due to regulations and innovations like index funds making investing easier and safer for most investors today than it was in 1890. Other research going back further in history for the US stock market finds that over the period 1792 to 1862, US stocks and bonds had more similar returns than the post 1890 sample where stocks are dominant. This does not invalidate the data and beyond the status quo, but it is worth considering that even this longdated data series does not represent the full range of possible outcomes for asset class returns. Additionally, this paper is a partial equilibrium model, meaning that it does not account for what would happen in equilibrium. If everyone took its advice and invested 100% in stocks. Other research suggests that if everyone switched from individual stocks and bonds to equity index funds, it could make investors in aggregate worse off. A partial equilibrium model is still useful for household decision-making as long as everyone doesn't actually decide to follow the advice. Finally, investor behavior needs to be accounted for. As we have seen, downside volatility is not the best measure of risk for long-term investors. But try telling that to your spouse when the market crashes. Even if bonds are relatively risky for long-term investors in real terms, their nominal volatility tends to be a lot lower than the volatility of stocks. Downside volatility aversion alone is probably enough to stop all investors from holding 100% equity portfolios in equilibrium, preserving the opportunity for those who can handle it. Thanks for watching. I'm Ben Felix, chief investment officer at PWL Capital.

have a higher standard deviation of outcomes but as the author points out the standard deviation indicates uncertainty about how much better off not worse off a retire will be after 30 years the author also comments that although a strategy that fully invests a retirement portfolio in stocks can be perceived as riskier it's kind of hard to call a strategy that has a low probability of failure provides similar downside protection and higher upside potential riskier than alternative strategies he also explains that however plausible sequence of returns risk is in theory it does not seem to have been a key determinant of portfolio failure in their broad Global sample this finding fits with a more recent paper with even more data to back up its conclusions the 2024 paper beyond the status quo a critical assessment of life cycle investment advice uses block bootstrap a method for simulating hypothetical data from historical data to simulate 1 million investor life cycles for an American couple who followed the 4% rule to spend from their portfolio in retirement the historical data source with simulations includes 39 developed countries with data as far back as 1890 and through 2023 spanning more than 2,600 years of country month return data the authors established an optimal 100% Equity portfolio allocated 33% to domestic stocks and 67% to International Stocks by testing all possible allocations to domestic stocks International stocks bonds and bills the tests evaluated wealth at retirement retirement income conservation of savings through retirement and bequest at death they then test this optimal all Equity portfolio against multiple life cycle asset allocations strategies including a portfolio of 60% domestic stocks and 40% bonds and a Target date fund with a decreasing Equity Glide path they find that the optimal all Equity portfolio dominates all other strategies the all Equity portfolio has a lower probability of running out of money under the 4% spending rule than the 6040 portfolio and the target day fund the au's also run a simulation where rather than picking an optimal fixed allocation for the full life cycle they allow this hypothetical couple to pick the optimal portfolio for each individual year year of the simulation in this case the optimal portfolio is again all Equity leading up to retirement but it shifts to a 27% allocation to bills a cash equivalent at retirement and then shifts back toward 100% equity in the following years the authors note that the modest allocation to bills early in retirement in this scenario is their simulated investors response to the sequence of returns risk that is created by the 4% rules fixed real withdrawal amount before anyone gets too excited about the idea of a cash cushion early on retirement stick with me until later in the video so far we've established that at least in the historical data a 100% Equity portfolio has looked pretty good relative to more conservative static allocations declining Equity Glide paths and Rising Equity Glide paths this flies in the face of sequence of returned risk as it is generally understood another popular strategy to deal with sequence of returns risk is the bucket approach or cash wedge holding a few years of cash to cover living expenses during Market downturns avoiding spending from the portfolio in those bad years in the 2019 paper the bucket approach for retirement a suboptimal behavioral trick the author explains that the approach is appealing to retirees because it sounds plausible it's psychologically comforting it's consistent with the well-known behavioral bias of mental accounting and it's easy to implement that all sounds nice but the paper proceeds to test whether the bucket approach has actually been helpful in historical data from 1900 through 2014 the author examines a two bucket strategy including a cash bucket with enough cash to fund two years of annual draws and a risky bucket consisting of a portfolio of stocks this is tested against various static allocations between stocks and bonds these strategies are then tested for a 30-year retirement period with retirement spending following the 4% rule across multiple performance metrics static strategies outperform the bucket strategies okay we've seen so far that Glide paths and cash buckets do not solve sequence of returns risk and that counterintuitively stock heavy static allocation portfolios tend to look better than Bond heavy portfolios Glide paths and portfolios with cash buckets that may seem discouraging for anyone worried about sequence of returns risk but I have good news all the retirement simulations discussed so far have used constant inflation adjusted spending following the 4% rule the 4% rule was developed by a financial planner William benan in 1994 great guy by the way you can hear him discuss his work on my podcast benan wanted to find the amount of a portfolio that could be spent in the first year of retirement and adjusted for inflation thereafter without running out of money using us historical data he found that a 4% initial withdrawal rate would have never exhausted a portfolio in under 33 years and deemed the 4% withdrawal rate safe for retirees safe withraw rates have become a focal point of retirement analyses including academic papers like those mentioned in this video and are ubiquitous in the financial planning Community the 4% rule does have lots of problems which I've detailed in other videos in short 4% % is probably not as safe as Ban's us Centric analysis suggests what most people don't realize is that before bengan Robert Merton had published papers on How to optimally consume from a portfolio and his solution was not constant inflation adjusted spending Merton's withdraw strategy use an amortization method that calculates the amount to withdraw in each individual year it's a little bit more complicated than a 4% rule which is probably why it didn't catch on in the same way let me explain how it works using Excel formulas in each year you solve for payment by inputting the portfolio's expected return the number of remaining periods and the portfolio value the payment is the amount that can be spent from the portfolio sustainably in that year based on the other assumptions the same calculation is done the following year and so on the result is a spending path that responds to changes in portfolio values changes in expected returns and the remaining time Horizon this approach solves some of the biggest problems with safe withdrawal rates safe withdrawal rates never adjust in a scenario like Alex bad sequence of returns this can mean running out of money sooner than intended amortization based spending spreads that risk out by reducing spending after bad returns and increasing it after good ones this means making some adjustments to spending along the way but minimizes the risk of catastrophically running out of money on the other hand in a good sequence of returns like Jim's safe withraw rates leave you with a huge pile of money at the end amortization based withdrawals address that issue along the way allowing you to spend more throughout your life this makes sense portfolios are variable trying to squeeze constant spending from a variable asset is bound to cause problems flexible spending transforms sequence of returns risk from a potential catastrophe Laten retirement to periodic spending adjustments throughout retirement remember in beyond the status quo a critical assessment of life cycle investment advice the authors find a brief optimal cash allocation early in retirement under 4% rule spending but when they test a flexible spending strategy not amortization based withdrawals but an approach that similarly responds to changes in portfolio values that the cash allocation disappears from the optimal portfolio the lesson here is that sequence of returns risk can be addressed through spending adjustments I might even say that sequence of returns risk is better described as sequence of withdrawals risk returns will be what they will be they are out of your control making the same inflation adjusted portfolio withdrawals through bad market conditions is a choice that puts unnecessary and potentially catastrophic strain on a portfolio flexible spending mitigates a bad sequence of returns and increases lifetime spending without increasing the risk of running out of money I built a model using Global stock data back to 1900 and found a 30-year safe withdrawal rate of 3.2% with a 0% failure rate this means that in the worst 30-year period in my sample which happens to be a period starting in 1913 a retiree could spend 3.2% of their portfolio in the first year and adjust for inflation thereafter without running out of money in the same data the worst case for amortization based withdrawals sustained average annual spending of 3.65% of the starting portfolio value without running out of money but it did require spending adjustments instead of a catastrophe later on some Sharp Cuts were made to spending early on and spending increased over time as conditions improved importantly under my very conservative 3.2% spending rule adjustments would not be made if returns turn out to not be the worst returns in history while under amortization based spending upward adjustments would be made each year it's important for me to say here that while static allocations to equity heavy portfolios have looked good in the historical data historical data do not predict the future Equity heavy portfolios can also be a challenge behaviorally asset allocation is not a decision that should be taken lightly or made based on a single piece of information if you want to learn more about choosing an asset allocation I've got another video on that topic sequence of returns risk often associated with investing in stocks during retirement is intimidating for retirees for good reason a bad sequence of returns can materially impact long-term retirement outcomes the problem is that most attempted asset allocation Solutions like Glide paths and cash buckets make expected retirement outcomes worse rather than better it is worth saying here that even if holding some cash is empirically suboptimal if it makes you feel better or more confident in your retirement plan it's probably fine statistically optimal and optimal for each individual person are not the same thing the most common portfolio withdrawals solution to sequence of returns risk the safe withdrawal rate produces extremely conservative and rigid spending rules amortization based spending or flexible spending more generally address sequence of returns risk or should I say sequence of withdrawals risk headon adjusting spending to market conditions and the remaining retirement period results in increasing total spending while decreasing the risk of running out of money thanks for watching I'm Ben Felix Chief investment officer at pwl Capital if you want to learn more about how pwl addresses sequence of returns risk through portfolio management and financial planning you can book a meeting with us below

## 24. The Case for Index Funds
**Channel:** Ben Felix | **Views:** 245K | **Date:** 11 months ago | **Duration:** 16:12 | **ID:** Nv5CiRSCVxA
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=Nv5CiRSCVxA

with a few exceptions most investors should be using lowcost index funds as their primary investment vehicle anyone who disagrees with that statement is probably misinformed conflicted or just plain wrong this is nothing you haven't heard me say before but I want to make the case for lowcost index funds in a single video that can be shared with people who are still investing in high fee actively managed funds as many Canadians are I'm Ben Felix Chief investment officer at pwl Capital and I'm going to make the case for lowcost index funds as the most sensible Investments for most people the case for index funds breaks down into six simple but important points each with supporting Theory and evidence cost efficiency diversification investment returns tax efficiency Simplicity and theoretical consistency before I get there I want to start with some background on index funds I also want to mention that I have some words of caution that I hope you'll stick around to here at the end of the video an index is a grouping of stocks designed to represent a market or Market segment for example the S&P TSX Composite Index is designed to be representative of the Canadian stock market S&P comes up with a list of company names and weights based on the relative sizes of the companies this is called a market capitalization weighted or cap weighted index the S&P 500 is similarly a market capitalization weighted index in this case representing 500 leading us companies a cap weighted index will always have more weight in larger companies like RBC and Shopify and Canada or apple and Microsoft in the US and less weight in smaller companies that you're less likely to have heard of market cap waiting is simple based on market prices and easy to implement which likely explains its widespread use in both benchmarking evaluating the results of another investment strategy and index investing creating funds that track an index an index fund is a mutual fund or ETF that tries to replicate the performance of an index usually by investing in the stocks in the index when I say Index Fund throughout this video I'm referring to cap weighted total market index funds index funds that aim to capture the returns of a total stock market as I'll explain later not all index funds are created equal to contrast an actively managed fund aims to outperform the market index by doing things like selecting a subset of stocks within the market and timing exposure to the market in an effort to outperform over the last 60 or so years index funds have gone from being a fringe idea and academic circles an idea that the asset management industry liked to criticize and even laugh at to becoming a mainstream investment vehicle this did not happen by accident a huge body of academic research starting around 1968 and continuing to today has shown that actively managed funds do not add sufficient value to cover their relatively High fees on average and that the vast majority of active funds underperform in the long run this brings me to the first point in making the case for lowcost index funds index funds have low fees the weighted average fee for index funds in Canada is 0.19% well for actively managed funds that do not have the cost of advice built in is around 0.85% those higher fees on average will translate to lower investment returns and no other identifiable benefits unless you really like supporting your fund manager research from Morning Star has found that fees are one of the best predictors of future fund performance vanguard's late founder John Bogle famously said that in investing you get what you don't pay for his point was that unlike other areas of life where paying more comes with the expectation of better results paying higher investment fees typically results in lower returns not higher a point corroborated by research from Vanguard formalizing this logic Nobel laurate Bill Sharp explained what he called the arithmetic of active management in 1991 if active and passive another name for index investing management styles are defined in sensible ways it must be the case that one before costs the return on the average actively managed dollar will equal the return on the average passively managed dollar and two after cost the return on the average actively managed dollar will be less than the return on the average passively managed dollar this suggests in simple terms that in aggregate investors in actively managed funds must underperform investors in total market index funds because active funds have higher fees and costs multiple academic Studies have found this to be approximately true before fees there is evidence that active funds add a bit of value on average but net of fees all credible studies agree that the average active fund is not adding value in addition to fees investors in a fund pay transaction costs in Canada a portion of these costs are reported as the trading expense ratio or teer index funds tend to have very low trading expense ratios often rounding to 0% while actively managed funds which typically trade more tend to have higher transaction costs it's not uncommon for the trading expense ratio of an actively managed fund to be higher than an index fund's fees and trading costs combined as important as fees and costs are a lot of people are not aware that low costs are one of the benefits of index funds only 31% of respondents to a 2022 survey of Canadian investors knew that index funds have lower fees and expenses than actively managed funds as important as they are fees are not the only reason that actively managed funds will tend to underperform their relative lack of diversification also poses a challenge this brings me to the next Point index funds are broadly Diversified by replicating the returns of a market index index funds hold a huge portion of the stocks in the market on the other hand in their efforts to beat the market actively managed funds will tend to hold fewer stocks portfolio concentration can be a good thing if a fund manager knows how to pick winning stocks but picking winning stocks consistently is really hard stock returns are positively skewed this means that most stocks don't perform very well in the long run while a relative few perform exceptionally well the result is that while the long-term Return of the overall Market is positive the majority of individual stocks underperform Based on data from 1926 through 2016 only 42.6% of US Stocks had lifetime Buy and Hold returns that exceeded one-month treasury bills just over 50% of US Stocks had negative lifetime returns about 12% of US Stocks lost 100% of their value and only 30.8% of stocks generated lifetime Buy and Hold returns that beat the market suffice to say that most stocks do not beat the market over their lifetimes the data for Global stocks are even more discouraging if a fund manager identifies those relatively few winners ahead of time they will generate High returns but they are much more likely to end up with losers index funds on the other hand always hold the winning stocks this idea was detailed in the 2017 paper why indexing Works which separate from fees helps to explain why active managers tend to underperform a benchmark Index this brings us to the next Point index funds beat the vast majority of active funds in a 2023 study looking at long-term returns for us Equity mutual funds from 1991 through 2020 the authors find that most funds underperform an index fund even before fees will a few funds win big this is important multiple studies including this one find that the average active mutual fund slightly outperforms before their fees but this study shows that most funds 54.8% of them in the sample underperform an index fund even before their fees are considered the explanation is that the skewness in stock returns makes it more likely for active mutual funds to pick losing stocks than to pick winning ones after fees only 30.3% of us Equity mutual funds in this study beat a US Equity Index Fund over the full sample other research like the S&P us Spa report finds even lower rates of outperformance for example for the 20 years ending June 2024 fewer than 6% of us Equity mutual funds in the spe report beat the S&P composite 1500 index the OG 1968 paper that introduced the idea of a valuating mutual fund returns on a risk adjusted basis and introduced the term Alpha to Define excess risk adjusted returns found that 115 mutual fund managers in the period 1945 to 1964 were on average not able to outperform the market in fact there was little evidence that any individual fund was able to do significantly better than what we would expect from random chance an influential 2010 paper similarly found that net of fees the average US Equity active fund underperforms and again that the distribution of fund returns is in line with what we should expect by random chance before fees but discouragingly worse when the higher fees of activ managed funds are taken into account a 2014 paper examines data on global Equity Funds and comes to similar conclusions active management is a zero sum game before feast and a negative sum game after fees these data suggest that you are much more likely to underperform than to outperform by investing in an actively managed fund though there's still a small chance of picking a big winner the obvious next question is whether you can identify those big Winners ahead of time a lot of investors look for active funds that have beaten the market in the past and hope that they're going to continue about performing in the future this is the question of persistence unfortunately there is little evidence that the best performing active Managers from a prior period continue to outperform in future periods there are a couple of potential explanations for this one as described in a highly cited 1997 paper that does not mince any words is that the data do not support the existence of skilled or informed mutual fund portfolio managers basically managers who were successful in the past were simply lucky and their luck eventually ran out another possible explanation described in a highly cited 2015 paper is that mutual fund managers are skilled but their skilled does not translate to higher returns for fund investors instead assets flow into the funds of skilled managers right up to the point that the fund gets too big for the manager to continue outperforming due to dis economies of scale the result result is that the most skilled managers earn huge fees by having large funds but the benefits of their skill ACR to them the fund manager not to their investors in either case luck or diseconomies of scale there is a lack of persistence this is important because there will always be some active funds that have outperformed in the past but that doesn't matter if you can't identify the future winners ahead of time in the long run index funds beat the average active fund and the vast majority of active f funds without the need to worry about whether you've picked a winning manager I've had people ask me why you would settle for average returns with index funds but the reality is that index fund returns will almost always be way above the average active fund net of fees index funds are also tax efficient more trading inside of an active fund will tend to lead to more taxable distributions which can be an additional drag on long-term returns research comparing before and after tax returns of actively managed funds and index funds has found that how ever bad things look for active management before taxes it looks worse after taxes for taxable investors one of the biggest but often overlooked benefits of index funds is their Simplicity if you invest in actively managed funds it is often difficult to evaluate how you're doing is the manager doing what they're supposed to be doing have they lost their Edge has their fund gotten too big with index funds you can be pretty confident that you will earn the return of the index net of some predictively low fees that is very easy to evaluate and to stick with similar to how you get what you don't pay for in investing you also tend to keep more of your returns by doing less this idea was popularized by The 1975 paper the losers game the author describes Winner's games where the outcome is decided by the winner and losers games where the outcome is decided by the loser winning a Winner's game requires exerting effort to win while winning a loser's game requires minimizing your mistakes to avoid losing investing is a losers game one of the author's prescriptions to avoid mistakes is to keep things simple something index funds accomplish easily the author wrote if you can't beat the market you certainly should consider joining it an index fund is one way the data from the performance measurement firms showed that an index fund would have outperformed most money managers the final point I want to make is that index funds are consistent with foundational Finance Theory Harry marwitz developed modern portfolio theory in the 1950s on the basis that optimal portfolios minimize variance for a given level of expected return or maximize expected return for a given level of variance this is accomplished through diversification maritz showed mathematically why diversification is so important bill Sharp's 1964 Capital asset pricing model or capm turned marwitz Theory into a testable prediction about the relationship between risk and expected return in the capm each asset's price is based on its contribution to the risk and expected Return of the overall Market one of the big insights from the capm is that in an efficient market the market capitalization weighted total Market portfolio is the portfolio with the optimal risk and expected return tradeoff whether markets are actually efficient enough for the market portfolio to really be the optimal portfolio is an important question well it's an ongoing debate Eugene F's work on market efficiency starting in the 1960s and continuing to today suggest that markets are efficient enough that most investors should act as if they are efficient even if perfect market efficiency is an unrealistic ideal to be really clear here perfect market efficiency is not something even F believes in on my podcast we asked F how we can know whether markets are efficient and he said that at least from the perspective of investors the fact that pref fee active mutual fund returns look roughly like what we would expect by random chance while net a fee active fund returns are a negative sum game is the strongest and most relevant evidence that markets are at least efficient enough that investors should treat them as efficient together the works of marwitz sharp and F all recipients of the Nobel Memorial prize and economic Sciences suggest theoretically that most investors should own the market through lowcost index funds rather than paying high fees trying to beat it an assertion supported by the evidence presented earlier in this video I do want to offer a word of caution I have been talking about total market index funds today there are more indexes than there are stocks think about that many of those indexes and the funds tracking them look more like actively managed funds than total market index funds this issue was detailed in a 2019 paper passive iname only delegated management and index investing not all index funds are good Investments one glaring example is thematic index funds which aim to capture the performance of hot investment themes like crypto AI or electric vehicles they tend to launch at the peak of investor excitement for the theme cannabis is another one and then go on to deliver staggeringly poor performance similarly using index funds to bet on Industries is probably not a good idea as I detailed in a recent video the type of index funds most investors should be looking for as supported by the theory and evidence presented in this video are capitalization weighted total market index funds I think it's also important to mention that people often take index funds to mean S&P 500 index funds well the S&P 500 represents a large portion of the US market and has performed incredibly well especially in recent history it's probably not the only index you want to invest in adding exposure to the rest of the US market and to stocks in other countries is probably sensible here in Canada we have a ton of asset allocation ETFs that deliver lowcost globally Diversified Index Fund portfolios in a single fund further simplifying the implementation of these sensible ideas index funds have low fees they are broadly Diversified and largely due to those two characteristics they beat the vast majority of actively managed funds most of the time and especially at long Horizons they're also tax efficient simple to understand and Implement and consistent with financial Theory Canadians have been particularly slow to adopt index funds I hope this video helps to change that thanks for watching I'm Ben Felix Chief investment officer at pwl Capital if you enjoyed this video please share it with one of the many Canadians who are still investing in high fee actively managed funds

## 25. Choosing an Asset Allocation (How Much in Stocks vs. Bonds?)
**Channel:** Ben Felix | **Views:** 213K | **Date:** 1 year ago | **Duration:** 13:06 | **ID:** p25PPBgMiEk
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=p25PPBgMiEk

### Transcript:
how much risk you take with your Investments is one of the most important determinant of your expected investment returns this makes choosing an asset allocation the mix between riskier stocks safer bonds and other Assets in a portfolio one of the most consequential decisions that every investor has to make but how much risk should you take and what even is risk I'm Ben Felix Chief investment officer at pwl Capital and I'm going to tell you how to choose an asset allocation [Music] I'm going to start off talking about risk as the risk of an investment declining in value but I want to say upfront that while this is typically how risk is described by the financial industry to investors it's not always the best measure of risk or at least not the only way to think about risk I'll come back to this point in the second half of the video assessing how much risk you should take has three main Dimensions that make up what is called a risk profile your behavioral loss tolerance that's your ability to sleep at night and not panic cell when a risky investment drops in value your ability to take risk that is your capacity to hold a risky asset without needing to sell it in the short term to fund your living expenses and your need to take risk that is the amount of risk needed to achieve the expected return needed to meet your goals behavioral loss tolerance is an important constraint on asset allocation we can talk about portfolio optimization sharp ratios expected returns and uncorrelated assets all day but you ultimately have to have a portfolio that you can stick with through good time times and bad and all portfolios have bad times investors tend to sabotage their own returns by selling low and buying High ideally having an asset allocation that you comfortable with Will mitigate some of those common behavioral issues behavioral loss tolerance is psychological and it's Unique to each individual we can break it down into six elements risk tolerance represents your willingness to engage in financial behavior with an uncertain outcome and the potential for loss risk preference represents your general reference to take more or less risk Financial knowledge represents your level of financial education training and experience higher Financial knowledge is typically associated with a higher behavioral loss tolerance investing experience is your experience with investing you can be financially knowledgeable but have limited experience with Investments specifically investing experience is generally associated with being more comfortable with risk risk perception is your subjective assessment of the riskiness of investing that is separate from the objective reality of it risk perception can be influenced by things like the media your social environment and a lack of understanding of financial Concepts people who perceive investing to be more risky tend to be less adaptable to Market volatility the final element is risk composure your actual behavior during difficult market conditions the only way to know your risk composure is to have lived through Market declines and they're accompanying narratives about why that crash is different some Financial professionals go as far as suggesting being relatively conservative until you have lived through a crash I don't know if I'd say that but it is worth keeping in mind that watching your Investments decline can be more stressful than you might think best practice for establishing your risk tolerance is using a psychometric assessment that is typically a questionnaire that has demonstrated reliability and validity in predicting the behavioral Tendencies of investors those words reliability and validity have specific meanings in this context reliability is the trustworthiness or consistency of a measure and validity is the degree to which empirical evidence and theoretical rationals support the adequacy and appropriateness of conclusions drawn from an assessment once a questionnaire has been created and studied for its reliability and validity its results can be used to rank people who take the test in the future relative to the findings from the study I'll put a link to a reliable and valid questionnaire in the description it's based on a 1999 study so the questions may feel a bit dated but the results are reliable and valid no questionnaire is going to be perfect and there are more upto-date ones out there but this one is a available for free on the internet from the researchers who initially created it psychometric risk tolerance assessment results are interpreted relative to other people's scores this means that the results place you on a scale relative to lots of other people in a relevant population who have also completed the assessment so while it will tell you how risk tolerant you are relative to other people it won't tell you how to invest your money at pwl we map a high risk tolerance score to a 70 to 100% Equity portfolio assuming that the investor has sufficient risk risk taking ability an interesting Insight from a large sample of hundreds of thousands of cases run through morning Stars risk profiler is that risk tolerance follows a normal distribution most people do not have an extremely high risk tolerance keep this in mind if you think that you do another interesting and related research finding to keep in mind is that men tend to overestimate their risk tolerance while women tend to underestimate it this finding holds when controlling for other factors linked to risk tolerance like age household income marital status and education the same study finds that risk tolerance was also overestimated by younger respondents and those with a graduate education behavioral loss tolerance is important because investing in a risky portfolio and then selling out after it does poorly can be extremely expensive not to mention the psychological discomfort you're likely better off in that case in a less risky lower expected return portfolio that you can stick with through bad times I would call behavioral loss tolerance a binding constraint on acid allocation you really don't want to take more risk than you can handle the other binding constraint on asset allocation is your ability to take risk even if you max out the behavioral loss tolerance scale you can't take much risk with the money that you need to use to pay rent tomorrow risk-taking ability is your ability to withstand declines in your portfolio value without compromising your lifestyle risky assets like stocks have positive expected returns but they can drop in value significantly over the course of months or even years risk-taking ability can be broken down into three main elements the time Horizon for the goal being funded the ongoing need for liquidity from the portfolio and your capacity to absorb a financial loss without compromising your standard of living all else equal a longer time Horizon lower liquidity needs and higher risk capacity imply higher risk taking ability for example if you have a time Horizon of less than 5 years before needing access to the full amount of money being invested or if you need access to 5% or more of the portfolio annually and if you have no other assets or income sources to maintain your standard of living your AB ility to take risk is low on the other hand if you have a time Horizon longer than 10 years no ongoing liquidity needs and other assets or income sufficient to maintain your lifestyle if this investment goes south like a pension for example you have a high ability to take risk your human capital your ability to work and earn income also contributes to your ability to take risk if you have stable employment income that is relatively unaffected by the broader economy your human capital is more like a bond and allows you to take more risk with your financial assets a classic example is a tene professor if on the other hand your income is unstable and has the potential to drop in bad Economic Times your human capital is more like a stock and allows for lower risk-taking with your financial assets all else equal the last dimension in assessing how much risk to take is your need to take risk behavioral loss tolerance and risk-taking ability are constraints on the amount of risk you can take but someone with a high loss tolerance and high risk taking ability may not need to take a lot of risk riskier Investments typically have higher expected returns someone who's very wealthy may not need to have a high expected rate of return in order to meet their long-term objectives in assessing your need to take risk you need to understand the rate of return required to meet your goal if your goal can be achieved with the expected return of a lowrisk investment it's worth asking why you would want to take more risk I'd also be cautious if your goal requires an extremely higher rate of return to achieve for example PW capital's expected return for a 100% Equity portfolio is just under 7% if you need a 20% return to achieve your goal it might make sense to revise the goal in discussing behavioral loss tolerance risk- taking ability and the need to take risk we've been framing risk as the possibility of losing money on an investment in the short term but that's not the only relevant measure of risk stocks are volatile they move up and down a lot day to day and year to year this is how risk is often framed but long-term investors are less affected by short-term volatility low stock returns tend to be followed by higher stock returns and high returns by lower returns that's called serial dependence or in this case negative autocorrelation and it decreases the risk of stocks for long-term investors bonds have historically exhibited positive autocorrelation meaning that bad Bond returns tend to be followed by more bad Bond returns typically related to periods of high and persistent inflation I need to specify here that I'm talking about nominal Bonds in the US tips or inflation protected bonds would would not have the same problem Canada is phasing out its real return Bond program so those are less relevant up here positive autocorrelation makes nominal bonds riskier at long Horizons than at Short Horizons less volatile assets like bonds also tend to have lower expected returns overall and to be occasionally devastated by periods of high inflation stocks are not an inflation hedge since they don't necessarily increase in value when inflation is high but they have historically been able to outpace inflation at long Horizons most of the time due to their higher EXP expected returns stocks are more volatile than bonds which is one way to think about risk but they've also been less likely to lose purchasing power at long Horizons which is another way to think about risk if we believe that these characteristics of stock and bond returns will persist in the future optimal portfolios for long-term investors would generally have higher stock allocations while behavioral loss tolerance and the ability to take risk are still important constraints on asset allocation long-term investors should understand how risk changes at long Horizons I think this connects back to the financial knowledge and investing experience inputs in assessing behavioral loss tolerance if you can withstand volatility in the short term there is a reasonable argument that higher Equity allocations are safer for long-term investors even for retired long-term investors but you have to understand this to withstand the higher volatility the 2024 paper investment Horizon serial correlation and better retirement portfolios models optimal asset allocation using historical data and finds that optimal portfolios for long-term invest investors have higher equity allocations for example the optimal Equity allocation for an investor with a lowrisk tolerance who's concerned about their inflation adjusted wealth moves from an optimal 20% in stocks at a one-year Horizon to 50% at a 20-year Horizon other research has come to even more extreme conclusions suggesting that despite higher volatility 100% stock portfolios are the least risky option for long-term investors including for retirees of course even if that is true behavioral loss tolerance is still a major constraint another important thing to think about is what kind of risk you are taking I often see risk-seeking investors trying to increase their returns by holding big positions in one stock or betting on one industry that's probably not the right kind of risk to take you expect a higher return for owning a diversified portfolio of stocks over bonds and that expectation must be there for investors to be willing to hold riskier assets that is called a compensated risk the specific risks of individual stocks or individual Industries are not compensated risks they're not risks with a positive expected return typically it makes sense to diversify away those uncompensated risks as much as possible rather than speculating investors with a desire to take on more compensated risk can do so by increasing their allocation to stocks relative to bonds increasing their allocation to riskier stocks like small cap value stocks allocating to other well-established expected return premiums using leverage or some combination of all of those things it's important to remember though that that risk is risk risk premiums don't always pay off over all time periods and leverage amplifies both good and bad outcomes it's possible that taking more risk leads to worse outcomes rather than better risk is multi-dimensional your psychological Constitution and your financial situation are constraints on how much risk you can take even if you can handle a lot of risk psychologically and financially you may want to dial it back if you don't need to take much risk to meet your goals importantly though the nature of risk changes at long Horizons even a conservative long-term investor should likely have an allocation to stocks long-term investors concerned with their inflation adjusted wealth may find that a higher allocation to stocks is contrary to popular wisdom safer than a more Bond heavy portfolio as long as it doesn't cause behavioral problems finally investors who want to take more risk should consider taking compensated risks rather than speculative once thanks for watching I'm Ben Felix Chief investment officer at pwl Capital if you want to learn more about how pwl helps Canadians choose and stick with long-term portfolios you can book and meeting with us below
