## 33. The Problem With Private Credit
**Channel:** Ben Felix | **Views:** 80K | **Date:** 1 year ago | **Duration:** 8:58 | **ID:** DQAr1DeDIro
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=DQAr1DeDIro

### Transcript:
as a portfolio manager and the head of research at pwl Capital I get pitched on a lot of financial products the one that I've been pitched on by far the most in the last few years is private credit funds private credit consists of loans similar to bonds except they're non-publicly traded loans created by non-bank entities like private credit funds or business development companies to fund private businesses this asset class is promoted to retail investors for its high yields and stable returns but as you can probably guess I'm skeptical I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at pwl Capital and I'm going to tell you what private credit looks like under its smooth and shiny veneer private credit is one of the fastest growing asset classes the dramatic growth has likely been driven by investor demand for high floating rate yields during a period of low interest rates and by tighten Capital requirements for banks pushing small and midsized companies to find alternative sources of credit the two factors combined investors seeking yield and companies needing non-bank lending has resulted in the growth that we've seen for this asset class the fastest growing part of the private credit Market is private credit funds these are funds that raise money from investors often retail investors and make Direct Loans to private firms typically firms that can't get bank loans due to their credit worthiness loan terms are negotiated individually with each borrower and the terms often contain features that traditional fixed income would not like a structured Equity component the interest rates on these loans are high the borrowers are too risky for banks which means that the loans already command a higher interest rate but private credit funds also have to build their High fees into the rate they charge a typical fee structure would be 1.5% plus a performance fee of 15 to 20% all in that ends up being around 3 to 4% in fees between lending to higher risk borrowers and containing Equity features like warrants private credit can start to look a lot more like Equity than like a bond but due to its nature as a private asset class that risk will not show up as volatility the result is what appears to be high returns low risk and low correlations to public markets private loans are not valued daily like a stock and don't have a secondary Market to establish a price so their reported returns will not reflect the true volatility in the value of the underlying assets additionally the valuations of the loans are highly subjective with different funds assigning materially different values to the same loan when private credit assets are marked down the marks will tend to be much more muted than a comparable publicly traded asset this ends up being important when evaluating the past performance and risk of the asset class smooth returns and generous valuations on private loans can make private credit look much more attractive than it actually is this starts to touch on the question that every investor should be asking before allocating to an asset class what risks am I taking and how do I expect them to be compensated Financial assets represent discounted future cash flows their value changes when discount rates or cash flow expectations change this is always true but in some cases like in private markets the asset is not valued every day and is not valued by the market one interesting perspective on the effect of this comes from a 2023 paper in the financial analyst Journal titled direct lending Returns the author looked at publicly listed business development companies or bdcs closed end funds that engage in direct lending and trade in the public market bdcs are relevant to private credit since they're required to invest at least 70% of their Assets in non-public equity and debt of us corporations and they've been shown to be good benchmarks for the returns of private credit funds to evaluate BDC performance they construct a benchmark consisting of small cap value stocks and leverage loans since these public assets explain most of the variation in BDC returns this just means that the publicly listed bdcs have returns that look similar to small cap value stocks and leverage loans an important Insight that we're going to come back to later they find that when BDC performance is evaluated based on net asset values those are the reported values of the underlying assets by the bdcs in their regulatory filings they outperform liquid benchmarks by 2.74 percentage points per year and they have high sharp ratios that actually sounds pretty good it's like you can take risks similar to publicly listed assets while earning significantly higher returns but the problem is that publicly listed bdcs don't always trade at their net asset values particularly during times of Market stress they tend tend to trade at a discount the market is a pricing machine that absorbs huge amounts of information and outputs a price when the market assigns a price below the reported net asset value it might suggest that the net asset value is not accurate on a market value basis bdcs do not outperform the liquid Benchmark this suggests that when properly benchmarked and properly priced the apparent excess risk adjusted returns of private credit funds May evaporate along similar lines a 2024 working paper risk adjusted the returns to private debt funds applies a cash flow-based method to form a replicating portfolio that mimics the risk profiles of a large sample of private credit funds the authors find that using both equity and debt benchmarks to measure risk a typical private debt fund produces an insignificant abnormal return to its investors this means that investors are not getting anything special for the risk they're taking in these funds their risk adjusted excess return is indistinguishable from zero they do find that if Equity is removed from the benchmarks private credit starts to look really good this again highlights the importance of proper benchmarking and suggests that anyone adding private credit to their portfolio as part of their fixed income allocation May really just be increasing their overall exposure to the equity risk premium the other interesting observation from this research is that gross of fees private credit funds have large and significant excess returns theoretically skilled fund managers will attract assets to their fund up to the point where they are no longer able to generate excess risk adjusted Returns the end result is that managers collect all of the benefits of their skill as fees and investors in their funds earn returns in line with the risk they're taking some research on private Equity has cheekily referred to it as the billionaire Factory because private Equity managers generate large fees for themselves while delivering net returns similar to public equities for their end investors in the case of private credit funds it does appear that they are able to apply skill in identifying negotiating and monitoring private loans to firms that could not otherwise raise Finance ing that is impressive it is skill they charge a rate to those borrowers that approximately equals the risk adjust of borrowing rate plus the fees that the private credit funds charge the end result in line with what theory would predict is that fund managers absorb the benefits of their skill as fees and the end investors in private credit funds end up taking a whole bunch of risk paying high fees and getting returns that they could get much cheaper with better liquidity using publicly listed assets I think it's fine if investors are fully aware of these realities and dive into private credit anyway but I think it's much more problematic when high- fee investment products are being marketed as safe and stable while having high expected returns retail investors may not have the tools or knowledge to evaluate the risks that they are taking let's not forget that in addition to the hardto measure risks that I've mentioned many private credit funds are illiquid illiquidity Is Not Great under any circumstances but private credit funds will tend to gate redemptions at times when liquidity is in high demand when I talk about these funds being risky and potentially IL liquid it's easy to dismiss my comments as alarmist but I'm not joking here in Canada there have been recent examples of private credit funds halting their cash distributions and gating redemptions and because private credit has been sold to retail investors who are often relatively unsophisticated many of these investors have been blindsided when that risk shows up romson investment Corp a large private lender has cut its distributions multiple times and Frozen redemptions as it deals with loan defaults and ninepoint partners LP has sto paying cash distributions and skipped its current Redemption window the period where investors are typically able to get some of their money out due to the fund having insufficient cash it makes sense when you think about it those highin floating rate loans to risky borrowers seem great when everything is going well but in recent history we've seen a sharp rise in interest rates making it harder for those high-risk borrowers to repay their loans resulting in a cash crunch for the private credit funds for most retail investors allocating to private credit will result in high fees confusion about the amount of risk they're actually taking and potential illiquidity in return for these unfavorable characteristics there is no evidence of a compensating excess risk adjusted return since it gets absorbed by manager fees an observation consistent with an efficient market for manager skill thanks for watching I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at pwl Capital if you want to learn more about how pwl decides whether to invest in asset classes like private credit you can book a meeting with us below

## 34. Will ARKK Recover?

in its relatively Short History the ark Innovation ETF arkk has had an incredible run and it has an incredible narrative it's investing in the future and you don't want to be left behind unfortunately Beyond a couple of huge years it has struggled to deliver on the narrative being left behind actually looks pretty good investors in arkk many of whom arrived late to the party have experienced dramatic ups and downs with an emphasis on Downs for most investors the fund crashed 77% between 2021 and 22 after taking huge inflows the question anyone still holding this fund must be asking or should be asking is whether it will recover I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at pwl Capital and I'm going to tell you what the data say about technology bubbles crashes and the ability of active managers to make a comeback the general thesis of arkk is to invest in disruptive innovation the areas of potential economic growth Arc has identified May well be revolutionary but the relationship between revolutionary Technologies and investment returns is not what many people think technology has persistently revolutionized the way that we live our lives for hundreds of years but revolutionary technology has consistently been associated with asset price bubbles extreme asset price increases followed by Price declines we had insurance and global trade in the 1720s canals in the late 1700s and early 1800s railroads in the mid 1800s electricity in the late 1800s huge advances in intangible capital measured by patents in the 1920s and more recently of course the internet of the 1990s there have been many smaller scale technology bubbles following a similar pattern bicycle company stocks in the UK in 1890 electronic stocks in the 1960s biotech in the 1980s and in recent history cannabis electric vehicles and clean energy have all followed similar Paths of extreme price increases followed by sobering declines one explanation for this persistent phenomenon is that as investors learn about a Technology stock prices associated with it increase rapidly on optimistic future cash flow expectations and relatively low sector specific risk in the event of successful broad adoption of the technology its risks become more systematic more connected to the overall Market driving stock prices down in other words the bubble behavior is explained by equilibrium asset pricing the other explanation suggests that investors get too excited pushing stock prices above their fundamental values this excitement is typically accompanied by a new era or this time is different narrative the second explanation is plausible since we know investors make extrapolation errors engage in intention induced trading and respond to sensationalized coverage from the media an analysis of 88 revolutionary Technologies over 150 years suggests that technology bubbles require uncertainty surrounding the Innovation the existence of pure play firms tightly coupled with the Innovation easy to understand narratives supporting the importance of the Innovation and the presence of novice investors fueled by over optimism and overconfidence check check check and check in the case of AR R KK one of ark's greatest strengths has been crafting an easy to understand narrative which won the hearts of many novice investors this consistent historical pattern makes investing in Revolutionary technology a questionable strategy unfortunately it's one of the most common mistakes that I see new investors make new investors specifically are relevant here because they have been found to be more prone to biases that lead to return chasing potentially contributing to the existence of bubbles in the first place they're more prone to extrapolating recent price movements more heavily influ by attention and more prone to hurting or following the crowd I definitely see this happen new investors tend to link expected economic growth to positive future investment returns which is intuitive but wrong the counterintuitive reality that expected economic growth and stock returns are somewhere between unrelated and inversely related due to the way that assets are priced is difficult to grasp asset prices reflect expected future cash flows discounted to today's dollars where the discount rate is related to risk Technologies with revolutionary potential have some combination of low discount rates and high expected cash flows driving their prices up eventually discount rates increase and cash flow expectations are brought back to reality causing the sharp drop that we see so consistently in the data Financial product manufacturers seem to be well aware that exciting themes attract investor dollars they strategically launch Niche products that coincide with the price runup of exciting themes but unfortunately for the investors funds tend to launch just after the peak of returns going on to underperform risk adjusted benchmarks I know I'm painting a negative pict here but technology bubbles are not all bad they provide lowcost capital for Innovative firms to implant new technologies in the economy for example just being added to an AI labeled ETF boosts the price of AI related companies driving down their cost of capital there have been similar effects in the past with other Technologies like adding do or blockchain to a company's name resulting in a temporary boost to its valuation while this can be good it also tends to attract lots of fraud even still asset price bubbles have historically been associated with the construction of technological infrastructure that ultimately benefits the economy but as we should expect investors supplying lowcost Capital have not been the ones to benefit and in many cases it ends up being inexperienced investors left holding the back based on the historical relationship between revolutionary Technologies and stock returns it seems unlikely that arkk will make a comeback on that basis but this ETF is actively managed even with the odds stacked against them maybe they are skilled enough to make a comeback there is a long history of star fund managers flaming out some recent examples from the dotc era are worth considering Garrett Von Wagner's emerging growth fund launched in 1996 and in 1999 he delivered a 291 per return attracting 56 million of new investor dollars by the end of the year mostly in November and December Von wagonner had a strong narrative he was in San Francisco he understood how Innovation was going to impact the economy he was even featured in a PBS Frontline episode in 1997 where viewers were told that no one had the Golden Touch more than him after the huge 291 per return in 1999 Von wagonner lost 21% in 2000 60% in 2001 and another 64% in 2002 he stuck with it until early 2008 but was unsuccessful at making a comeback another wild story is Ryan Jacob who took over the kinetics internet Fund in December 1997 he posted a 196% return in 1998 and a 216% return in 1999 Jacob started his own Fund in 2000 the Jacob internet f fund the fund lost 79% in 2 56% in 2001 and 13% in 2002 $100,000 invested in the Jacob internet Fund in 2000 would have been worth $88,000 by the end of 2002 incredibly and impressively got to hand it to him Jacob is stuck with it the fund even returned 123% in 2020 though it gave up a lot of those gains in 2022 since Inception the fund has returned 0.73% annualized through March 2024 I know these are just two examples so looked at 114 actively managed mutual funds that existed in 2000 still exist today and suffered a draw down of 70% or more at some point over that period draw down is the maximum Peak to trough decline that fund investors experienced the draw Downs for most of these funds unsurprisingly had Peaks starting in either 2000 or 2007 many of them are sector funds focusing on specific sectors like financials technology and energy but many are growth funds the average time to recover from a 70% or greater draw down in my sample is just over 14 years and this does not account for inflation or the opportunity cost of not being in some other investment over that period the other thing is that not all of the funds have recovered out of 114 funds in my sample 28 or about a quarter of them have yet to recover one of the behaviors that may be related is risk shifting if a manager has performed poorly in the past they will take on more risk in an attempt to improve their performance and attract more assets this is an interesting agency problem because the relationship between performance and flows is asymmetric funds with Ste performance attract inflows from investors while poor performance is not equivalently penalized this creates an incentive for fund managers to take big risks with poor risk return tradeoffs by increasing their weight in high beta stocks or concentrating their portfolio in a few risky stocks or Industries empirically funds that risk shift in an attempt to improve their performance generate significantly negative Alphas despite these data many investors will hold on to losing funds in hopes that they will recover there are a few explanations for this Behavior one is that disposition effect It is Well documented that investors hold on to losing Investments longer than they probably should due to loss aversion mental accounting pride and regret another is the endowment effect investors Place more value on an asset that they already own so even if they hold something they wouldn't buy today they may continue to hold it I find one of the most useful exercises to overcome this issue is to imagine that you did not own the asset in question say it's arkk if you did not own it today and you had the cash instead would you invest invest that cash in arkk the story arc for the arc Innovation ETF so far follows a path that is well trodden throughout history exciting Technologies increase related stock prices dramatically some fund managers in that space end up delivering incredible performance and look brilliant well doing it and then their performance falters along with the asset price bubble deflating I can't predict the future I don't know if arkk will make a comeback but the weight of evidence on revolutionary technology as an investment thesis active manager ability to recover from large draw downs and the Agency problems with risk shifting suggest that it would not be wise to count on it thanks for watching I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at pwl Capital if you want to learn more about how pwl helps people avoid investing in funds like arkk and trust me that is a conversation I had many times when arkk had its years of high returns you can book a meeting with us below

## 35. Budget 2024: Capital Gains Tax is Increasing [Canada]
**Channel:** Ben Felix | **Views:** 85K | **Date:** 1 year ago | **Duration:** 8:10 | **ID:** QyCQGuXdmcs
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=QyCQGuXdmcs

### Transcript:
Canada's federal budget 2024 has proposed an increase in the capital gains tax rate in certain cases this means that selling a taxable asset like a business a secondary real estate property or an Investment Portfolio May cost more to make things more interesting capital gains realized before June 25th will be taxed at the old lower rates this opens the door for Smart Financial Planning and the potential for mistakes I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at pwl Capital and I'm going to tell you what an increased capital gains tax rate means for your Investments a capital gain arises when you sell an asset for more than its adjusted cost base which is what you paid for the asset plus any expenses related to acquiring it if you buy a stock for $10,000 and later sell it for $115,000 you have a $5,000 capital gain capital gains in Canada were not taxed at all prior to 1972 since 1972 the top combined Federal and provincial capital gains rate for individuals in onario has been as high as 39% in the 1990s and as low as 23% in the 2000s the 2024 budget proposal would set the top capital gains tax rate for individuals in Ontario at a little more than 35% the variation in the capital gains tax rate over time is largely due to changes in the capital gains inclusion rate in Canada the full capital gain is not included in your taxable income the amount that is included is dictated by the capital gains inclusion rate this is what the budget is proposing to increase the inclusion rate has been 50% since 2000 it was 75% from 1990 through 1999 66% in 1988 and 1989 and 50% before that the overall tax rate on a capital gain is simply the inclusion rate multiplied by your income tax rate while the capital gains inclusion rate and by extension the tax rate on capital gains is proposed to increase the first $250,000 of gains earned by an individual will be taxed based on the old 50% inclusion rate corporations which is where many small business owners and professionals do their investing are getting hit harder they do not get the $250,000 limit they pay tax at the new higher rates on their first dollar of capital gains corporate taxation in Canada is complex but by my calculations a capital gain realized in a corporation and flowed through to a shareholder will now be taxed at a total rate of 38.6 2% compared to the previous 28.97 per. again corporations do not get the $250,000 of capital gains at the old inclusion rates like individuals do for individuals many people will be protected by the fact that they can realize up to $250,000 of capital gains per year at the old 50% increase inclusion rate but this will likely still catch lots of people with second properties and larger investment portfolios and it will be particularly impactful in the year of death of the second spouse when all personal gains are realized employee stock options are similarly affected by these proposed changes if you have qualified employee stock options they previously received a stock option deduction for half of the stock option benefit and that is being decreased to onethird for amounts over $250,000 that $250,000 limit is shared by stock option income and capital gains the question a lot of people are asking is whether they should do anything before June 25th while they still have access to the 50% inclusion rate with no limit let's think about a basic example if you as an individual have a taxable asset worth $1.1 million and an adjusted cost base of $100,000 you have a $1 million capital gain I know those seem like big numbers but this could be an Investment Portfolio a family Cottage or a rental property paying the capital gains tax on that asset prior to June 25th would result in about 268 ,000 of tax at the top rate in Ontario paying the tax after June 25th would result in about $335,000 of tax that is obviously a big difference but I'll tell you why it's not obvious that everyone should be selling their taxable assets before June 25th selling now well resulting in a lower tax bill today also means that you have to pay that lower tax bill today if you're planning on selling the assets soon anyway it makes sense to do it on June 23rd instead of June 26th if you are not planning on selling though it's much less obvious deferring that tax bill into the future even knowing that it will be at a higher inclusion rate can still make sense if the opportunity cost of paying the tax now is high enough the opportunity cost will be a function of your expected investment returns and your tax rate we've been running these numbers at pwl trying to figure out what makes sense for people to do we set our modeling up to give us the break even year as an output this means that if you were planning on deferring your capital gains for some number of years the break even number of years you're better off deferring the gain rather than realizing it immediately at the current lower rates for example an asset worth $1.1 million with an adjusted cost base of $100,000 held by someone at the top Ontario tax rate with an expected return equivalent to a 100% Equity portfolio is better off deferring that capital gain for at least 7 years than realizing it today if they would otherwise sell before 7 years they're better off realizing the gain before June 25th to lock in the lower tax rate to be clear I am not suggesting that people go out and sell assets today this is very new information Professionals in tax and financial planning are still digesting the proposed changes and figuring out what to do next it's also a proposed change not yet law if it does pass into law we have until June 25th to figure it out so there is no immediate rush though to be fair some assets like businesses and real estate can take quite a while to sell it is important for people to understand the impact on their specific situation and plan accordingly let's not forget that for long-term investors real in large capital gains is not something that you're doing every year or at least it shouldn't be this will be even more true after June 25th but building tax efficient investment portfolios has always been important this is yet another reason that lowcost and low turnover Investments like index funds are useful tools actively managed funds tend to trade more and distribute more capital gains each year due to having higher portfolio turnover index funds don't trade much inside of the fund so they distribute very little capital gains this budget proposal does highlight one of the many reasons that just by vro is not a financial plan the literal action of purchasing a lowcost ETF has become very easy and it's an important step in implementing a broader financial plan but there's a lot more to consider financial planning considers how an investment interacts with things like retirement planning tax and Estate Planning and budgeting for large future purchases all of those items interact with an increased capital gains inclusion rate and may affect investment decisions the other thing that this highlights is tax rate risk we talk a lot about return UNC C but after tax returns are what put food on the table and changing tax rates can affect after tax returns an example of something that we do to mitigate this at pwl is realizing capital gains to use up clients low tax brackets in years that they have room available this helps to lower the eventual terminal tax bill and it reduces the risk of changes in future tax rates like the ones that we are seeing now similarly for people with corporations we typically recommend taking money out of the corporation to at least fund registered accounts like rrsps tfsas and RPS this results in tax diversification having money in multiple buckets with different tax treatment these little details are all part of the broader scope of portfolio management and financial planning and they can make a big difference in the long run I like to say that investing has been solved which is largely true a great portfolio is pretty easy for anyone to buy but investing is only one sliver of Good Financial Planning budget 2024 has proposed an increase in the capital gains inclusion rate capital gains realized after June 25th will have an inclusion rate of 2third as opposed to the previous one half on amounts over $250,000 for individuals and on any amounts for corporations this change will increase the top Ontario tax rate on capital gains from 26.7 7% to 35.6 n% for individuals and from 28.97 to 38.6 2% for people investing in their corporations in some cases it may make sense to realize capital gains before June 25th to get ahead of the rate increase but for many long-term investors this will do more harm than good thanks for watching ing I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at pwl Capital if you want to learn more about how pwl applies tax awareness and long-term financial planning to portfolio management you can book a meeting with us below

## 36. The Most Important Lessons in Investing
**Channel:** Ben Felix | **Views:** 199K | **Date:** 1 year ago | **Duration:** 7:02 | **ID:** MOjS2zuQMdo
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=MOjS2zuQMdo

### Transcript:
over the last 10 years I've spoken to thousands of individual investors about their Investments and financial plans while working at pwl Capital and I've interviewed some of the smartest people in finance economics and psychology on the rational reminder podcast the lessons I've learned from those experiences are hard to condense down into a single video but I'm going to do my best these are all lessons that investors will learn eventually though many will learn them the hard way I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at pwl Capital and I'm going to tell you what I think are the most important lessons in investing you're not that smart relative to the market you might be really smart but it's not good enough to be smart if you want to beat the market you have to be smarter and faster than the competition and the competition is in aggregate smarter than almost every individual this time is always different crashes and Bubbles are persistent features of financial markets and they're exacerbated by narratives The Narrative is always different but some things don't tend to change most notably returns after crashes are more often positive than negative and new parad bubbles tend to have disappointing Returns the market is forward-looking the market is constantly predicting the future and Market participants have an incentive to be the first to bring information to the market even then no single participant can have all the relevant information most of the time a piece of information that you think gives you an edge will already be reflected in prices market forecasts are not useful they're more often wrong than right and they feed into investor psychology paying attention to Market forecasts and worse yet acting on them will not improve improve the quality of your investment decisions and will likely lead to unnecessary anxiety time in the market beats timing the market investors often fail to capture market returns due to poor timing decisions sticking to a disciplined strategy will typically be more effective than trying to time the market especially after costs and taxes most funds do not beat the market we know from fund flows that investors Chase funds that have been successful in recent history it hopes that the performance will continue in reality funds that have been successful in the past are no more likely to be successful in the future some evidence even suggest that recently successful funds are more likely to go on to have poor performance and the vast majority of actively managed funds do not beat the market over any time Horizon incentives matter content creators have an incentive to make exciting often fear inducing content Financial product salespeople have an incentive to sell their financial product and financial advisors have an incentive to make investing seem more complicated than it is none of this means that there can't be good content creators salespeople or financial advisers but as a consumer of information it's important to understand how incentives may be affecting the information that you receive expected economic growth and stock returns are unrelated this is one of the most persistent misconceptions I see among investors particularly inexperienced investors the idea is that if you invest in the highest growth company sector or country your investments will outperform the reality is much more nuanced expected growth is reflected in current prices if that growth materializes as expected investment returns for the asset will align with its risk if growth is better than expected returns can have a positive surprise if it's worse a negative surprise the result is a somewhat random relationship good portfolio management does not make up for bad financial planning investing is a means to meeting financial goals but investment returns are not a financial plan a good portfolio won't tell you how much to save how much Insurance to buy how to reduce your taxes or how to structure your estate risk and expected returns are positively related this is important for two reasons if you want to earn higher expected returns you need to be willing to take more risk and if someone tells you that they can get you high investment returns with no or low risk you should be skeptical related to the last point the risk expected return tradeoff changes based on your time Horizon due to stock returns being somewhat mean reverting stocks are a little bit less risky at long Horizons and due to inflation being persistent safe assets like bonds and cash can be risky at long Horizons feeds and taxes matter lower fees are one of the best predictors of future Fund performance but fees are only part of the story more active strategies tend to be less tax efficient when the tax costs of active management are considered even fewer funds outperform complexity and costs are positively related more complex Financial products tend to have higher fees and costs and evidence suggests that complexity makes investors worse off rather than better in investing simple lowc cost Solutions will tend to dominate more complex ones there is no single optimal investment strategy people agonize over the perfect portfolio but everyone has different objectives constraints beliefs and preferences two people can have different portfolios with both being optimal for the respective situations yes even dividend investors the best investment strategy for you is the one that you can stick with sticking with an investment strategy through bad times is a requirement of capturing his benefits but it's one of the hardest things for investors to do most investors underperform the funds that they invest in and increasingly so with more volatile funds there's no such thing as a passive investment there's a spectrum of how passive or active a strategy is but nothing is truly passive since every investment is active on some level it's important to understand which active decisions you're making and why increased wealth does not give you access to Market beating Investments the supposed benefits of Investments marketed to wealthy investors like hedge funds private equity and private credit are likely more fairy tale than reality they typically come with high costs they're opaque and their economic benefits are not well supported diversification is still the only free lunch and investing diversification has been called the only free lunch and investing for decades because it offers the rare opportunity to reduce risk measured by a return variance without reducing expected returns investment should be evaluated on process not outcome people can get lucky or unlucky with an investment outcome especially in the short run to avoid chasing recent returns it's important to understand why an investment was made and what its expected outcome looks like probably the most important lesson is is that investing has been solved nobody fails to meet their goals because they didn't have tail Risk insurance or Structured Products in their portfolio for most people investing in a portfolio of lowcost total market index funds is good enough even if it's not perfect the good news is that many though not all of these lessons can be acted on by simply investing in lowcost total market index funds the problem is that a lot of these lessons are related to why it can be hard to keep your head down and stick to a strategy even a simple one successful investing is simple but it's not easy the one lesson that index funds are not the answer to Is that good portfolio management does not make up for bad financial planning you can't buy an index fund and automatically have your long-term goals mapped out Quantified and acted on with a plan that incorporates tax and estate planning if you want more color commentary on these lessons we discuss them at length in episode 299 of the rational reminder podcast thanks for watching I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at pwl Capital did I miss any lessons that are important to you tell me about them in the comments

## 37. Why Taking CPP Early Is (Usually) A Mistake
**Channel:** Ben Felix | **Views:** 62K | **Date:** 1 year ago | **Duration:** 10:52 | **ID:** r9vYji99fhk
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=r9vYji99fhk

### Transcript:
the Canada Pension Plan benefit is one of the most valuable retirement assets for most Canadians but its value can change materially depending on when the benefit is claimed this makes planning to get the most out of it a valuable exercise fortunately research at the intersection of financial planning and Actuarial science provides a lot of insight into optimizing the CPP timing decision I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at pwl Capital and I'm going to tell you how to get the most out of your CPP benefits for most retirees in Canada deciding when to claim CPP benefits is one of the most consequential financial decisions that they will make the difference between a good choice and a bad choice can measure in the hundreds of thousands of expected lifetime dollars let's start with some fundamentals the CPP benefit is calculated as a percentage of the maximum pensionable earnings or MPA the mpa is the average of the trailing five years of ymp or yearly maximum pensionable earnings if you're already sick of acronyms buckle up more recently the ym was also introduced historically the maximum CPP benefit what we would today call Base CPP was simply 25% of the mpa however recent CPP enhancements are gradually introducing a second earning ceiling and increasing the replacement rate from 25% to 33.33% the way this breaks down is that the first additional CPP maximum benefit once it's fully implemented is 8.33% of the mpa so 25% plus 8.33% brings us to 33.33 % of the mpa and then the second additional CPP maximum benefit once it's fully implemented is 33.33% of the mpa adjusted difference between the Y MP and the ymp if that made your heads spin don't worry about it too much these additional benefits will only have a small impact for people retiring in 2024 and will be fully implemented by 2064 the important point to understand here is that the CPP benefit is based on the mpa the maximum pensionable earnings numbers that I mentioned are increased each year based on Canadian wage growth which has historically outpaced inflation by about 1% each year once the CPP benefit starts it is indexed to the CPI all items index the important Point here is that the CPP benefit is initially calculated based on the mpa which is indexed to wage growth well once the benefit has been claimed it is indexed to Consumer Price inflation with that background out of the way to understand how the CPP timing decision can be optimized it's important to understand how timing affects the benefit there are two main channels one of them is relatively well understood but the other is not the easy one is the statutory increase or decrease in the CPP benefit for claiming it earlier or later than age 65 the CPP benefit can be claimed anytime between the ages of 60 and 70 there's a 0.6% reduction for the total benefit each month prior to age 65 for a maximum reduction of 36% at age 60 and a 0.7% increase for each month after age 65 for a maximum increase of 42% at age 70 this means that if we take age 65 as the Baseline and you're entitled to the maximum base CPP at 25% of the mpa you could increase that figure to as much as 35.5% of the mpa by delaying to age 70 and you could decrease it to as little as 16% of the mpa by claiming at age 60 that aspect of deferring CPP is relatively well understood people generally get that there's a penalty for taking it early and a bonus for deferring it but the other Factor affecting the lifetime benefit is way growth remember deferring gets you a larger percentage of the mpa let's take a 65-year-old as an example if they take CPP today assuming that they have sufficient contributions for maximum base CPP at 25% of the mpa in 2024 they would get about $6,000 in CPP benefits if they defer to age 70 two things will happen they will get a statutory increase of 42% and the mpa will increase with wage growth assuming 1% real wage growth the increase in the life time benefit relative to an age 65 Baseline is 49.2% instead of a $16,000 benefit it would be about a $24,000 benefit that $24,000 is adjusted for inflation to be directly comparable to getting $16,000 today getting this increase does however require waiting for 5 years which means drawing down other assets instead of waiting it's common for financial advisers to tell their clients to take CPP at age 60 so they can avoid touching their Investments a 2020 paper from the Canadian Institute of actuaries and the Society of actuaries suggest that for Canadians with sufficient bridging funds from RSP or riff retirement savings the choice to delay or not delay CPP payments comes down to expectations about longevity and financial market returns the paper finds that if a risk free investment with a rate of return of 1% above inflation is available anyone who expects to live past age 80 should defer CPP to age 70 rather than take it at 65 the authors note that only a fifth of female CPP recipients and a quarter of males die before age 80 under current CPP Actuarial tables in the case of a risky investment the paper finds that assuming a 4% rate of return with a 4% standard deviation roughly in line with pwl capital's expectations for a fixed income portfolio a 65-year-old male with high expected longevity would face a 73% probability of receiving less lifetime net income by claiming CPP at age 65 instead of age 70 for women who tend to live longer the paper finds an 81% chance of being worse off from having taken CP P at 65 instead of 70 they also look at a 6% expected return in line with pwl capital's expected return for a 70% stock in 30% Bond portfolio and find a 57% chance of being worse off for men and 65% for women higher expected returns and lower expected longevity would make taking CPP earlier look better all else equal but even in the high expected return low longevity case the paper suggests a 51% chance that a male ends up worse off and a 60% chance that a female ends up worse off from having taken CPP at age 65 rather than age 70 the paper concludes that the majority of Canadians with sufficient Savings in their RSP or riff have a high probability of making themselves better off in the long run by deferring the CPP benefit to age 70 despite the clear and measurable benefits of delaying the CPP benefit as of 2023 only a little more than 5% of Canadians deferred to age 70 the majority claimed the benefit at age 60 or age 65 there are a few possible explanations for this Behavior a simple explanation is that people don't know any better a 2018 survey conducted on the government of Canada website found that only 36% of Canadians aged 40 to 64 were aware that they could defer their CPP benefit to receive an increase another possibility is that CPP advice is misguided one of the most common approaches to examining the CPP timing decision is through Break Even analysis for example if you take CPP at age 65 instead of age 60 you'll need to live long enough to collect the higher benefits to make that deferral worthwhile Break Even analysis is affected by the fact that people tend to underestimate their own longevity and the fact that the break even framing makes deferring the benefits seem like the risky option empirically Americans presented with break even analysis are more likely to claim Social Security as early as possible while it's casually appealing Break Even analysis ignores or underemphasizes the benefits of CPP remember the CPP benefit is a guaranteed inflation indexed annuity it protects against adverse scenarios like bad market returns High inflation and a longer than expected lifespan an alternative approach approach to break even analysis introduced in a 2020 paper is the lifetime loss which takes the present value at life expectancy of the expected loss from having taken CPP at an earlier age rather than a later one this approach flips the framing to early claiming being a loss and more appropriately captures the lifetime value of CPP another possible explanation for early CPP claiming is our old friend conflict of interest a 20123 paper in the retirement management Journal finds using us data on Social Security claiming behavior that household with financial advisers who are paid on commission tend to take Social Security earlier than those with hourly or fee based advisers their results suggests that to nobody's surprise financial advisor compensation methods can have a significant impact on the scope and quality of financial advice while this video has so far focused on the arguments to defer the CPP benefit there are cases where it does not make sense to do so an obvious example is people who do not have any funds to bridge the gap between ages 60 and 70 another important consideration is life expectancy those with lower life expectancies would generally want to claim earlier than later but importantly many people underestimate their own life expectancy this decision should be made carefully and not based on a casual assessment of life expectancy another example is lower income Canadians eligible for the guaranteed income supplement they have to be very careful about increasing their taxable income past age 65 as the effective tax rate that is the tax rate including clawback government benefits can be well over 50% in these cases taking CPP at age 60 is often The Right Move though all cases should be considered based on their specific facts similar comments can be made about old age security clawback to the extent that deferring CPP will result in OAS clawback careful consideration should be made about whether deferring actually makes sense another interesting case occurred in 2022 that would have favored taking CPP before age 70 remember that the CPP benefit is based on the mpa at the time that you claim which changes based on wage growth and is then indexed to CPI once the benefit has started the benefit of deferring consists of two parts the statutory increase and wage growth which typically increases faster than inflation but in 2022 we had high inflation measured by CPI and low wage growth the result was that someone age 69 would have been better off claiming in 2022 getting a year of CPP benefits plus a big jump from CPI indexing the following year then waiting until age 70 in 2023 this example goes to show that rules of thumb are always a challenge in financial planning the CPP uptake decision is one of the most consequential financial decisions that most Canadian retirees will make the difference between optimal and suboptimal claiming can measure in the hundreds of thousands of expected lifetime dollars in general deferring to age 70 is the wisest move but there are many exceptions including ones that I did not mention in this video at pwl Capital we model the CPP decision in comprehensive financial planning software that accounts for taxes government benefits and variable investment returns in this modeling we routinely see the benefits of delaying the CPP benefit in terms of a higher expected estate State value and a higher probability of meeting retirement income goals thanks for watching I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at pwl Capital if you want to learn more about how pwl incorporates the CPP claiming decision into our financial planning process you can book a meeting with us below before you go I want to give a shout out to Jason ye from fineo solutions for providing feedback on this video Jason has great animated videos explaining CPP on his channel

## 38. CPP is More Valuable than Most People Realize
**Channel:** Ben Felix | **Views:** 62K | **Date:** 1 year ago | **Duration:** 7:38 | **ID:** hEgdz4-Jm2g
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=hEgdz4-Jm2g

### Transcript:
in 2025 combined employee and employer Canada pension plan or CPP contributions will be approximately 8,848 that is a lot of money for most Canadians whether this is good news or not is a question everyone should be asking I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at pwl Capital and I'm going to tell you whether CPP is a good deal for Canadians I posted about CPP on Twitter a few weeks ago and some people were infuriated that I would say anything positive about it people don't like feeling like the government is controlling their savings but the reality is that CPP is one of the most valuable retirement income assets for most Canadians CPP is a social insurance plan designed to contribute to retirement incomes in Canada it's not designed to be the only retirement asset but it is designed to be one of the pillars in a retirement income plan for many years CPP was designed to replace 25% of income up to a maximum called the yearly maximum pensionable earnings but by 20125 contributions will go toward future benefits with a replacement rate of 33.33% up to an increased maximum this enhancement is funded by an increase in required CPP contributions which began in 2019 and will be fully implemented by 2025 this is objectively good news but people often think that they could do better investing CPP contributions on their own that CPP won't be there for them in the future or that their contributions will be wasted due to an early death each of these objections shows a lack of understanding of the benefits of CPP the CPP benefit is an inflation indexed annuity that is the only truly truly risk-free asset for a long-term investor inflation index annuities are not available for sale privately in Canada which makes sense because they're extremely risky contracts for a private insurer to issue this makes CPP extremely valuable it's so valuable because CPP Hedges three of the most important risks that retirees face longevity risk inflation risk and sequence of returns risk these are not risks that an individual investor can efficiently hedge on their own longevity risk is the risk of outliving your retirement assets the longer you live the more you need to be able to draw from your savings to fund your living expenses each additional year of life is an additional year of expenses empirically the risk of depleting a portfolio of stocks and bonds before death increases with increasing expected lifespans meaning that more individual savings are required for a longer life but annuities address longevity risk in a different way rather than needing to save more to protect against longevity risk pooling the longevity of many people together allows for the risk to be shared when CBP contributors live longer than expected they gain what are often referred to as mortality credits from the contributors whose lives ended earlier than expected hedging longevity risk is not something that any individual can accomplish on their own but it is accomplished very efficiently in annuities like CPP inflation risk is the risk that the purchasing power of your assets is eroded by inflation the biggest risk to retirees in historical data has been inflation risky assets like stocks and to a lesser extent bonds May provide sufficient returns to outpace inflation in the long run but they do not tend to offer an inflation Hedge the implication is that while your Investments may eventually outpace inflation they will not necessarily increase in value when inflation is high putting pressure on the ability of your assets to fund the rising cost of living High inflation tends to have catastrophic effects on the long-term real returns of bonds and cash the same assets that investors typically consider safe the CPP benefit on the other hand offers true inflation indexing each year the benefit for those receiving CPP is increased based on the CPI all items index an interesting point on CPI indexing is that it may overstate actual increases in the cost of living by as much as 0.6% per year due to things like substitution that is people changing their spending habits in response to inflation like longevity risk inflation risk is extremely difficult for any individual investor to hedge on their own CPP on the other hand is in a much better position to deal with inflation risk CPP contributions are increasing with wage growth which has historically outpaced inflation by about 1% per year and CPP invest ments the crown corporation that invests the funds held by CPP is able to invest in long-term often illiquid assets which would be unsuitable or unattainable for most individual investors the so-called Canadian pension fund model employed by CPP Investments is quantifiably successful at hedging real long-term liabilities through thoughtful Investment Management in addition to inflation risk individual investors drawing down a portfolio of risky assets may be adversely affected by severe Market declines or by high inflation and low real investment returns drawing large amounts from a portfolio of assets during periods of low returns can be challenging to recover from unlike a retire CPP Investments has an extremely long time Horizon and it has ongoing funding from contributions it is in a much better position to deal with Market volatility than individual investors are the guaranteed income stream from the CPP benefit plays a role in reducing sequence of returns Risk by providing a baseline level of income through all market conditions despite all these benefits people often seem to think that they will be able to do better investing on their own but matching the benefits of CPP is not easy to do it's important to understand that as a hedge asset an asset that delivers a high rate of return under specific adverse scenarios like high inflation or a longer than expected life it should not have a high expected return in normal conditions and it's not a high-risk asset that could go to the Moon one way to assess the rate of return of CPP contributions is using the internal rate of return or irr the rate of return that sets the present value of all contributions equal to the present value of all benefit payments the IR will depend heavily on the mortality and inflation assumptions used in a simple example with maximum contributions based on the full 2025 contribution rates and the expected maximum benefit being received in 2064 the real irr that is adjusted for inflation at a normal life expectancy is a little more than 2% remember that is adjusted for inflation assuming 2.5% inflation we're talking about close to a 5% return on contributions yes it is true you would have a higher expected return investing in stocks pwl assumes an average nominal return of 7.08% for a globally Diversified index ETF portfolio but the whole point of CPP is that it will do well in scenarios where realized stock returns are lower than expected inflation is higher than expected or you live longer than planned the other thing that doesn't get enough appreciation is that by providing a hedge against sequence of return and inflation risks CPP allows investors to take more risk with their other Financial assets than they could otherwise increasing their overall expected returns for this reason comparing CPP to stocks makes little sense at longer life spans the irrs on CPP contributions increase significantly particularly when the CPP benefit has been deferred resulting in an increased benefit this is particularly relevant for some demographics who have meaningfully longer life expectancies yes if you pay into CPP for your working life and then die after one CPP benefit payment you would have been better off with private savings that is true but if you live to be 100 you will benefit greatly that's how mortality pooling works it's not theft it's insurance I do understand that people don't like the government controlling their savings but CPP is pretty great the CPP benefit is the only inflation index annuity available to most Canadians it offers protection against some of the most significant risks that retirees face and it does so in ways that individual investors are not able to accomplish on their own this also makes planning to get the most out of CPP of valuable exercise which is a topic I'll cover in a future video thanks for watching I'm Felix portfolio manager at pwl Capital if you want to learn more about how pwl incorporates CPP into retirement planning you can book a meeting with us below

millions of Canadians walk into Bank branches every day hoping to get advice but instead they get a sales pitch for products that they may be better off without Canada's banks are incredible businesses but that doesn't mean they give incredible Financial advice in fact evidence suggests it may even mean the opposite I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at pwl Capital and I'm going to tell you based on the CBC report as many of them are stressed out by the pressure to sell at all costs based on some of the answers given on mutual fund fees and expected returns I suspect that Beyond conflict of interest a lack of Education contributes to the quality of advice some of the bank financial advisers captured by cbc's investigation did not seem to know how mutual fund fees work and others had to conflict of interest but research on financial advice suggests that many financial advisers just don't know any better a 2021 paper in the Journal of Finance finds in a Canadian sample of financial advisers and their clients that advisers typically make the same mistakes personally as they do in their client accounts they trade too much they chase returns they prefer expensive actively managed funds and they underd Diversified in this study the advisor's personal accounts underperform just as poorly as their clients accounts the study also shows that advisers don't strategically hold expensive portfolios only to convince clients to do the same because the advisers continue to do so after they leave the financial services industry this suggests that conflicts of interest are only part of the problem and highlights the importance of seeking out Financial advice from people with appropriate credentials like the CFA and the cfp credentials don't guarantee good advice but they are definitely a valuable filter good credentials also come with Professional Standards and codes of Ethics which are enforced by the issuing bodies which is important because conflicts of interest are definitely still a problem Bank employees are clearly saying that conflicts of interest are affecting the advice that they give to clients this is also supported by academic research a 2018 paper in the review of financial studies uses data from a large retail bank to analyze how Banks and their financial advisers generate profits from customers the authors find that advised transactions earn the bank higher profits than independently executed transactions placed by the same client unsurprisingly the bank's own mutual funds and Structured Products are most profitable for the bank and Bank profits increase with trade size also unsurprisingly Bank advisers recommend exactly those transactions if these transactions were making clients better off there would be no issue to talk about but the research finds that advised clients have worse performance than unadvised clients suggesting that advisers put their employers interests first all of this is problematic for the many Canadians who do walk into the bank branch looking for financial advice I've done that too before I started my career in financial services I walked into a bank branch when I got my first job and asked what I should do with my savings I was of course directed into the bank's High fee actively managed mutual funds that was a long time ago but times have not changed the important next question to ask is where people should go to get advice acknowledging my own biases independent wealth management firms like pwl Capital are generally a better option than Banks I can't speak to other firms but I can tell you that pwl does not tie compensation to sales targets everyone on our team has paid a competitive salary and they're evaluated on the advice that they give not the products that they sell which is the way that it should be I said acknowledging my own biases but my own biases are what led me to PW in the first place I think Financial advice should be free from conflict of interest I started investing it was a light bulb moment for me but those products don't pay the commissions that I needed if I wanted to pay my rent so yes I'm biased to say firms like pwl are good but it's a ## 40. Do Stocks Return 10% on Average?
**Channel:** Ben Felix | **Views:** 462K | **Date:** 1 year ago | **Duration:** 8:43 | **ID:** Yl3NxTS_DgY
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=Yl3NxTS_DgY

### Transcript:
a lot of people seem to think that stocks will return 10% or more per year on average I think this belief is misguided and can lead to bad financial decisions I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at pwl Capital and I'm going to tell you where the 10% return figure comes from why it's probably not a good estimate of expected returns and what a better estimate looks like the assumptions that investors make about expected returns impact important decisions like how much they need to save how they allocate their assets and whether they should invest or pay off debt small differences in expected returns can have big impacts on decisions this makes estimating expected returns an important exercise but many people seem to believe that stocks will return 10% or more per year on average to understand why this is problematic let's start by understanding where the idea that stocks return 10% per year on average comes from going back to 1950 through 2023 US Stocks delivered a nominal before inflation annualized return of 11.32% for the 20 years ending 2023 the total US market returned 9.81% annualized the Genesis of that often quoted 10% figure is easy to see especially for people primarily familiar with recent US market history before going any further we need to make a distinction between nominal and real returns it doesn't actually mean much to say that the stock market returns 10% on average because nominal returns don't put food on the table real returns do take the 15 years ending April 1985 as an example the US Stock Market returned 10.58% annualized for 15 years which seems great but inflation ran at 7.05% over the same period eroding a huge portion of the nominal return for this reason it's important to think in real terms the real return on US stocks from 1950 through 2023 was 7.63% and it was 7.16% for the last 20 years to be clear this roughly 7% real return is still exceptional even for the US market from 1900 through 1950 US Stocks returned a real annualized 5.57% the big question is whether the pre-1950 sample or the post 1950 sample are more relevant in setting expectations for the future to make this determination context matters a significant portion of the exceptional return for US Stocks over the more recent period is due to rising valuations from 1950 to today US Stock valuations increased dramatically Rising valuations are not something that investors should count on repeating valuations are kind of like a weak form of gravity in financial markets High valuations suggest lower expected returns not higher the question of whether pre or post 1950 returns are more representative of expected returns was examined by Eugene F and Ken French in a 2002 paper I know the paper's a bit dated but US market valuations are similarly high today as in their analysis period they estimate made expected stock returns from dividends and earnings to judge whether the realized average return is high or low relative to its expected value they find that their estimate for the expected Equity risk premium for the period 1872 to 1950 is very close to the realized Equity risk premium over the same period but that the 1951 to 2000 realized risk premium is almost three times their estimated premium this suggest that the earlier period delivered on expected returns which should inform expectations while the later period delivered unexpectedly higher returns which should not be counted on expectation their evidence suggests that the higher return post 1951 is due to Rising stock valuations driven by Falling discount rates this context is important because falling discount rates indicate lower expected returns going forward not higher the idea that Rising valuations drive up past returns and push down expected returns while investors assume that the high returns will continue forever has been called the rear view mirror bias a more recent paper examining past us stock returns suggests that survivorship has contributed a significant amount to the historical us Equity premium basically the US has been lucky disasters that could have happened to the US and have happened to other countries simply have not happened as a result investors have learned over time that the US market is safe this learning has driven down the discount rate driving up the valuations of US Stocks the combination of good luck and valuation increases together explain about 2% of the historical us Equity risk premium for the period 1920 through March 2020 the thing about the us being a great place to invest is that everyone knows that it is prices for US stocks are high reflecting how awesome investors think the market is but again high prices suggest lower expected returns not higher for future returns to resemble the past there will need to be more good luck more rising valuations or some combination netting the 2% return from luck and learning out of the total return the real return on US Stocks over the period examined by the paper is 5.28% a figure much closer to the pre-1950 US return and as you will see in a minute to Global stock returns historical US Stock returns have been high enough to be deemed a puzzle the equity premium puzzle they're much higher than the predictions of most economic models one of the ways that researchers have tried to resolve the equity premium puzzle is by looking at data outside of the US market Global real stock returns back to 1900 through 2023 excluding the US market are 4.35% and 5.16% including the US market research drawing on data for 38 developed markets extending as far back as 1890 and using block bootstrap to simulate plausible return scenarios finds a median real 5.28% return for international stocks and 4.78% for domestic stocks remember the often cited 10% or higher average nominal return for stocks is roughly equivalent to saying a 7% % or higher real return this 7% figure is about 2% higher than unbiased estimates of us expected returns US Stock returns before 1950 and Global stock returns for periods spanning 1890 through 2023 to answer the question posed in the title of this video no stocks do not typically return 10% or more per year on average US Stocks specifically did for the last 70 or so years largely in the back of rising valuations but Global stock returns for the last 124 years and US Stock returns from 1900 through 1950 were much lower at pwl Capital we need to have estimates for expected returns to give people Financial advice rather than using relatively recent us historical returns and ignoring valuations we start with the global historical return from 1900 through 2023 remove the return over that period attributed to valuation changes and then account for current valuations we update these figures twice a year and post them on our website following this process gives us a real expected return of 4.62% or a nominal 7.24% assuming 2.5% expected inflation these figures are before fund fees which should also be accounted for net of fees pw's expected return for a total market index fund portfolio is a nominal 7.08% the difference between a 10% and a 7% expected return is huge it may not seem huge but remember that returns compound over time with small changes in expect itive returns differences in things like how much you need to save for retirement how much you can spend in retirement and when you can retire can be huge I do want to make sure it's clear that I'm not suggesting that stocks are bad Investments or that people should avoid the stock market in all of the long-term historical data that I've mentioned and in pw's expected returns stocks have beaten and are expected to beat bonds the main takeaway is not that stocks are bad just that their expected returns are not as high as many people think using one of the best historical periods for one of the best performing stock markets as an estimate of expected stock returns especially when valuations for that market are currently at the 97th percentile of expensiveness relative to history a condition associated with lower future returns is in my opinion a little ridiculous more reasonable estimates include the historical experience of global stocks and account for current market conditions both of these adjustments point to lower expected returns relative to recent US Stock Market history fortunately pwl runs these numbers twice a year and posts them on our website for free thanks for watching I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at pwl Capital if you want to learn more about how pwl applies sensible expected return estimates to financial planning and portfolio management you can book a meeting with us below
