## 41. Is Home Country Bias a Mistake?
**Channel:** Ben Felix | **Views:** 98K | **Date:** 1 year ago | **Duration:** 10:34 | **ID:** jN8mIHve1Ds
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=jN8mIHve1Ds

### Transcript:
international diversification is theoretically and empirically important but investors all over the world exhibit a home country bias in their portfolios they tend to overweight the stocks in their home country relative to their country's market capitalization weight for example Canada makes up around 3% of the global market but Canadians tend to allocate more than 50% of their portfolios to Canadian stocks home country bias is often viewed as a bad thing and at the extremes it probably is bad but there are some good reasons to overweight the stocks in your home country I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at PWL Capital and I'm going to tell you why home country bias can be a good thing in moderation in basic financial theory under the capital asset pricing model the market capitalization weighted portfolio is the optimal risky portfolio for all investors this suggests that an investor in Canada should hold Canadian stocks at their market capitalization weight of around 3% the fact that investors around the world significantly overweight their home countries is known as the home bias puzzle a puzzle like this can mean that investors are making persistent mistakes or it can mean that investors are acting rationally for reasons not reflected in the model being used to define the puzzle the capital asset pricing model makes lots of assumptions that don't reflect the real world it doesn't account for costs and taxes it ignores how the portfolio interacts with things like the cost of buying groceries in your specific country and it's silent on how the treatment of foreign investors during times of crisis can affect investment outcomes in Canada the fees to own Canadian stocks through low cost index funds tend to be lower than the fees to own foreign stocks and investors receiving dividends from a Canadian corporation get credit for the taxes that the corporation has paid on the other hand a Canadian receiving a dividend from a foreign company gets no credit for the taxes paid by the foreign corporation this increases the relative tax cost of owning foreign stocks for Canadian investors all else equal favorable cost and taxes make Canadian stocks a little bit more attractive for Canadian investors but probably not enough to explain the large observed home country bias a later iteration of the capital asset pricing model the ICAPM or intertemporal capital asset pricing model suggested investors don't only care about how their portfolio behaves in a vacuum they care about how it behaves a relative to other stuff that matters to them like buying maple syrup maple syrup was a bit of a Canadian joke but in all seriousness to the extent that the Canadian companies are producing cash flows that provide better Protection against changes in the prices of local goods that people in Canada care about some home country bias can make sense the empirical evidence discussed later in this video does seem to support this effect a less often discussed problem that can arise with international diversification is the treatment of foreign investors international diversification looks great when things are going well and global capital markets are free and open as they largely are today but major world events like wars can cause markets to close up and foreign investors don't tend to get the best treatment in the early 1900s many countries around the world had active financial markets and European investors were being encouraged in popular investment books like we see today to diversify their portfolios internationally French, British, and German investors exported huge amounts of capital around this time in search of returns and diversification through cross border investment starting with World War 1 global financial markets began to contract between market closures foreign exchange restrictions and at the extreme market nationalizations foreign investors experienced a lack of access to their investments and the potential for meaningful or even total losses this type of experience is not only an artifact of history either Argentina instituted restrictions on the flow of capital in 2019 which continue to be a challenge for foreign investors and Russia put restrictions in place in 2022 that affected the ability of investors in deemed unfriendly countries to transact in the shares of Russian companies these types of problems are avoided by investing in domestic stocks this is a point that Professor Eugene Fama made when he was on the Rational Reminder podcast and it always stuck with me the problem is nobody cares about investors investors get expropriated and so you know each side always expropriates the other side's investors but they don't fix it after the war it stays expropriated even if you win so uh that that's the risk of international investing and it's not gone it's not gone one other point worth considering is that people care a lot about their relative economic standing given the fact that many Canadians have a home country bias being the one Canadian without a home country bias when Canadian stocks are doing exceptionally well could be a psychological challenge it was not that long ago that Canadian investors had to be convinced to invest outside of Canada because Canadian stocks were doing so well and the Canadian dollar was appreciating making returns for foreign stocks look that much worse all of the preceding points are important but another interesting angle to take on home country bias is purely quantitative what do the data say? I think the largely qualitative points that we just covered are really important but I'll be the first to admit that I get excited about data a recent paper that I covered in a previous video found in a large sample of developed markets dating back to 1890 and through 2019 that a 35% allocation to domestic country stocks has been optimal over the full life cycle of saving for and funding retirement 35% of the portfolio in domestic stocks performed better than other domestic allocations suggesting a home country bias for people in most countries the US being the big exception the data set up in this paper uses a block bootstrap methodology meaning that they pull multiple samples of real historical returns from a large data set and then string them together to form hypothetical return experiences the benefit of this approach is that it allows the researchers to simulate, in the case of this paper, 1 million possible return experiences drawing on the actual set of historical returns that countries around the world have seen in their setup domestic does not refer to any specific country but to the simulated returns of a representative domestic country it is important to note on this paper that they look at domestic allocations from 5 up to 100% and while 35% was optimal the differences in outcomes were relatively small between a 5% and a 50% domestic allocation while the differences did become much larger in a bad way at a 100% domestic allocation importantly these figures do not account for the generally more favorable costs and taxes when investing domestically with these relatively small differences in mind I think there's an element of reasonableness and preference that go into the home bias decision but some level of home bias does seem to be sensible at least based on that research I do have two more research findings to share Vanguard looked at optimal home country allocations using minimum variance analysis for Canadian investors in a 2023 paper using data from 1999 through May 2023 and they found that a 30% allocation to Canadian stocks was optimal I looked at Canadian dollar real returns for global portfolios with various allocations to Canadian stocks from 1900 through 2022 and similarly found that an allocation to Canada between 30 and 40% produced the best risk adjusted returns some caution is warranted with findings like Vanguard's and mine because they're specific to the time periods being examined but it is somewhat reassuring that these results line up with the bootstrap simulations mentioned earlier which attempt to address that exact issue one note on all of these quantitative findings is that they are based on having a home country bias in developed market countries either a representative developed market or Canada the conclusions may not extend to people living in emerging markets I'm pretty comfortable saying that there are strong theoretical practical and empirical arguments for having some level of home country bias in equity portfolios but a common concern with overweighting a small market like Canada is that may go on to have low realized returns I get the concern but empirically the stock returns of smaller countries where size is measured by total market capitalization tend to be higher than larger ones it is certainly possible that any individual country could have a bad long term outcome which along with the previously mentioned empirical evidence supports the argument to limit home country bias but I don't think it's an argument to eliminate it another common concern is that [insert country name] has poor economic prospects this one is coming up right now for Canada it's always important to remember though that economic growth and realized stock returns are not as closely related as many people believe in fact they're somewhere between unrelated and negatively related the reason is that expected economic growth is reflected in stock prices rather than stock returns being tied to economic growth they're tied to how actual economic growth compares to expected economic growth a very noisy often random relationship additionally in our currently global economy many countries have stock markets that derive a meaningful portion of their revenue from foreign countries no country is an island foreign revenues don't explain all of local stock returns which is one of the reasons that we diversify globally despite the existence of multinational companies but they do explain a meaningful portion of returns economic prospects aside investors in some countries including Canada may be concerned that there are just too few companies for the market to deliver a reliably positive equity risk premium but I don't see much of a relationship between the number of companies and the realized returns of country indexes in fact the best performing developed country index since 1970 Denmark only has 16 holdings home country bias overweighting your home country's stocks relative to their capitalization weights is detrimental at the extremes but modest home country bias is theoretically practically and empirically useful it can reduce fees and taxes it may hedge the cost of local consumption and it reduces exposure to the potential mistreatment of foreign investors when times get tough it may also be helpful psychologically due to the role of social comparison in determining individual happiness empirically a home country allocation of around 35% has been historically helpful in improving risk adjusted returns and life cycle outcomes for investors in developed markets even lower domestic allocations which still represent a home bias for investors in countries outside of the US have been better than having no home country bias in Canada there are a ton of options for low cost asset allocation ETFs which typically do have a home country bias at PWL Capital we typically allocate one third of equity portfolios to Canadian stocks thanks for watching I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at PWL Capital if you're in Canada and want to learn more about how PWL approaches portfolio management and financial planning you can book a meeting with us below

## 42. Why It Might Be Time To Rethink Lifecycle Asset Allocation
**Channel:** Ben Felix | **Views:** 158K | **Date:** 2 years ago | **Duration:** 8:56 | **ID:** JlgMSDYnT2o
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=JlgMSDYnT2o

### Transcript:
conventional wisdom and popular personal financial advice suggest that portfolios should contain at least some bonds and that asset allocations should shift increasingly into bonds as investors move toward retirement investors often use rules of thumb like 100 minus their age in stocks or they use target date funds which systematically shift from stocks into bonds over time this seems sensible given the perception that portfolio risk should decrease as retirement nears and progresses but it may rest on flawed assumptions I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at PWL Capital and I'm going to tell you why it might be time to rethink traditional asset allocation advice asset allocation is the mix of stocks bonds and other assets that make up an investment portfolio different assets tend to have different return characteristics and tend to interact with each other in different ways for example stocks and bonds don't usually move up and down in lock step asset allocation should also take things like human capital the ability to earn income into account most life cycle asset allocation models suggest portfolios containing a mix of stocks and bonds with the allocation to bonds increasing as people age this advice is consistent across textbooks industry courses and popular personal finance books and it's codified in target date funds which manage trillions of dollars of investors' assets fundamental to this advice is the idea that volatility in investment returns measures the risk of an investment risk is a complex topic but for long term investors while volatility is certainly important psychologically it may not be the best measure of risk risk should be measured at least in part as the probability of not meeting your future consumption goals the extent to which volatility is risk for a long term investor largely depends on how volatility is related to expected returns if your stock portfolio drops by 20% you will feel sad but if it's expected return increases when the portfolio drops your ability to meet your long term goals may be relatively unaffected empirically stock expected returns do seem to increase when stock prices fall major crashes are often followed by positive returns this mean reverting behavior reduces the risk of stocks for long horizon investors the question that follows is whether stocks provide better outcomes than bonds and cash in asset allocation for long term investors one of the keys to answering this question is dealing with the limited amount of available data on asset class returns while maintaining any mean reverting tendencies that may exist in the long term stock returns that have actually happened this work has been done in a recent paper titled Beyond the Status Quo a Critical Assessment of Lifecycle Investment Advice to model asset class returns the authors use a comprehensive dataset of historical returns on domestic stocks international stocks bonds and bills that spans 38 developed countries and nearly 2,500 years of country month return data from 1890 to 2019 they use these data to simulate 1 million possible return scenarios importantly their sampling method maintains the long term time series return characteristics of stocks and bonds the authors model the life cycle of a US couple who saves 10% of their income during their working years and consumes during their retirement years they begin to save at age 25 and they retire at age 65 receiving Social Security income and drawing down their portfolio using the 4% rule for retirement spending with his data set up the authors compare a bunch of asset allocation strategies on retirement wealth accumulation retirement spending the sustainability of retirement spending and net worth at death the big finding from the research is that rather than the traditional equity glide path represented by a target date fund or a stocks minus age strategy or even a constant allocation to 60% stocks and 40% bonds a portfolio of 35% domestic stocks and 65% international stocks for the full life cycle that means from early savings right through retirement is optimal across all measures domestic stocks in this data setup refer to a representative domestic country where domestic is largely referring to stocks denominated in local currency for example a Canadian investor would allocate 35% to Canadian stocks but an investor in the Netherlands would allocate 35% to Eurozone stocks this does suggest a significant home country bias for investors in many countries including Canada that's a topic for another video it's not surprising that an all equity portfolio results in better wealth accumulation but the finding that all stock portfolios continue to produce better outcomes throughout retirement is surprising it's not only the average outcome for retirees driving the result either the globally diversified all equity portfolio produces better left tail or worst case outcomes for total retirement consumption and a lower chance of running out of money this holds true at 3 4 and 5% portfolio withdrawal rates one of the other surprising findings is that even adding 5 or 10% in bonds to the all equity portfolio something that many investors would consider sensible from the perspective of diversification result in worse outcomes in the simulations one of the reasons is that while stocks and bonds have low correlations at short horizons suggesting a diversification benefit at long horizons domestic stocks and bonds have relatively high correlations similarly while bonds are less volatile they have much worse long term downside risk in terms of purchasing power and they don't tend to recover after crashes the same way that stocks do I think the main insight from this research is not that stocks are safe for long term investors they're still very risky but that bonds can be even riskier over long horizons like 30 years bonds become riskier than stocks more correlated with stocks and have worse downside risk than stocks another interesting point is that the dominance of all stock strategies is increasingly pronounced at longer life spans in the simulations where the probability of ruin for the longest lived couples in the model is much lower for couples investing in stocks than those investing in target date funds I know this is all making stocks sound really good but there is an important trade off the all equity portfolio in the simulations is more volatile and has much larger drawdowns on average which could be particularly difficult for retirees to endure psychologically we know from other research that investors behave really badly with volatile assets the gap between investor returns and investment returns that is what investors tend to lose due to inopportune timing decisions in risky assets tends to increase with volatility assessing how much risk as measured by volatility you can take involves broadly speaking an assessment of your psychometric risk tolerance your capacity to endure short term declines in the value of your financial assets and your composure through past experiences with market volatility it's great to say that an all stock portfolio performs well over the life cycle and it does but the price you pay is increased volatility and drawdowns which can manifest as a psychological cost but also as a real drag on performance if it results in bad investor behavior financial advisers do have some interesting interactions with these findings some empirical evidence suggests financial advisers boost the share of stocks in investors' portfolios and increase their ability to stick with their portfolios through major downturns contrary to traditional portfolio diversification and life cycle investing advice evidence drawing on data from a comprehensive survivorship bias free sample of stock and bond returns suggest that portfolio consisting of 35% domestic and 65% international stocks implying a home country bias for non US investors dominates balanced portfolios age based asset allocation strategies and target date funds on achieving wealth accumulation retirement consumption and bequest objectives before people run off and dump their bonds I have a few comments to finish with we can't know how representative these historical data are of the future it's possible that the equity risk premium the expected return premium for holding riskier stocks over safer bonds has decreased over time as markets have become more accessible and better regulated it's also possible that the time series characteristics of returns that were carefully preserved by the sampling method in this paper may not persist in the future lastly a lot of people simply don't want to live through 50% draw downs so where does this leave life cycle asset allocation I think investors at all life stages need to understand that volatility is not the only way to think about risk and that less volatile assets like bonds and cash may be very risky for long term investors it's also important to understand that psychological tolerance for volatility should not be taken for granted when looking at data like these there is a lot of uncertainty in estimating expected returns and the future will never feel certain risky assets are risky and their prices will tend to drop when scary things like pandemics or financial crises are happening you need to be comfortable living with your portfolio every day knowing that its long term outcome cannot be known with those caveats I think it makes sense to use data like these to shift the distribution of expected outcomes in your favor thanks for watching I'm Ben Felix Portfolio Manager at PWL Capital if you're in Canada and want to learn more about how PWL approaches portfolio management and financial planning for long term investors you can book a meeting with us below

## 43. The Problem with High Stock Valuations
**Channel:** Ben Felix | **Views:** 120K | **Date:** 2 years ago | **Duration:** 5:54 | **ID:** EybXQmxIq6U
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=EybXQmxIq6U

### Transcript:
I'm noticing investors worrying about stock market valuations being too high when stock market valuations are higher expected future returns are lower lower future returns can show up slowly over long periods of time or they can show up quickly with a stock market correction either way high valuations are not great for long term investors so to an extent the current concern is warranted but saying that stock market valuations are high misses a lot of nuance which stocks are we talking about and even more broadly which markets I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at PWL Capital and I'm going to tell you why the stock market is not as overvalued as many people seem to think US stock market valuations are high not the highest they've ever been but still very high. typically high valuations are related to lower future returns the relationship is messy noisy and can be violated for long periods of time but it does tend to hold at least eventually high valuations have preceded major crashes in the United States and in other markets the Shiller cyclically adjusted price earnings ratio or CAPE the ratio of stock prices to smoothed ten year real earnings reached peaks in 1929 and 1999 in the US followed by major crashes valuations in Japan got outright ridiculous in 1990 leading up to a crash that the Japanese stock market is still recovering from more than 30 years later there are good reasons to be worried about current valuations which are hovering around the level that preceded the 1929 crash this doesn't mean that the market will crash tomorrow but it does suggest lower expected returns for US stocks the inverse of the CAPE ratio the Shiller earnings yield suggest a real expected return of about 3% for the S&P 500 that's not great but it's also not the whole story the expensiveness of the US market is driven by a small handful of the largest stocks using a different valuation metric the price to book ratio the US market is expensive relative to history but the market overall less so than the S&P 500 and a lot of the expensiveness is coming from the so-called Magnificent Seven Apple Microsoft Alphabet Amazon Nvidia Tesla and Meta value stocks the stocks in the market with relatively low prices compared to fundamentals don't look all that expensive in many of the historical periods where stocks with high prices have gone on to have long periods of poor performance value stocks stocks with low prices have typically perform much better for the ten year period ending February 2,010 the S&P 500 Index lost an annualized 0.31% the NASDAQ 100 lost an annualized 7.86% and the MSCI US Investable Market Value Index returned an annualized 4.56% the single worst time to retire in US stock market history was December 1968 from then until January 1984 the S&P 500 gained an annualized 7.24% not bad but barely beat inflation which ran at an annualized 7.22% US large cap growth stocks returned an annualized 5.54% losing a ton of purchasing power over that period over the same period the dimensional US market wide value index returned to 12.31% annualized a more extreme example is Japan from 1990 through December 2023 the Japanese stock market measured by the Dimensional Japan Market Index delivered an annualized return of 1.22% obliterating the purchasing power of an investor in Japanese stocks Japanese growth stocks fared even worse losing an annualized 0.73% over this period over the same period Japanese value stocks returned 4.27% annualized the point I'm trying to make is that while the valuations in some parts of the US market are currently high the whole US stock market does not have high valuations tilting away from US gross stocks toward US value stocks which is what ETF providers like Avantis and Dimensional Fund Advisors do makes valuations less concerning the other thing to consider is that while the US market is expensive the US is not the world international developed and emerging market stocks have much more attractive valuations than the US US stocks look invincible right now after years of dominance over international stocks but a lot of those excess returns have come from rising valuations it's not clear that US dominance will continue into the future and in terms of expected returns markets other than the US look far more attractive believing the high past returns for the US market will continue into the future without considering US valuations is something known as rear view mirror bias US stock market valuations are high leading some investors to be concerned about a crash well it is true that US market wide valuations are high a lot of that is being driven by the biggest and most expensive stocks smaller and cheaper stocks within the US market do not have concerning valuations and historically have done well when the most expensive stocks have fallen back to earth it's also important to remember that the US stock market is not the stock market international and emerging market stocks have much lower valuations and correspondingly higher expected returns a US investor with a value tilt or a globally diversified investor have fewer reasons to be concerned about high valuations thanks for watching I'm Ben Felix Portfolio Manager at PWL Capital if you're in Canada and want to learn more about how PWL approaches portfolio management and financial planning you can book a meeting with us below I know this information makes it tempting to time exposure to certain types of stocks based on their valuation ratios but this is much easier said than done timing approaches including using valuation ratios rarely generate reliably positive access returns relative to just staying invested for timing strategies that do work they're typically highly sensitive to the time periods and strategy design being tested simply staying invested in a broadly diversified portfolio with exposure to multiple priced risks is more effective than attempts at timing them

## 44. Why ESG Investing May Be Doing More Harm Than Good
**Channel:** Ben Felix | **Views:** 52K | **Date:** 2 years ago | **Duration:** 7:20 | **ID:** c4AMFicXXqg
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=c4AMFicXXqg

### Transcript:
ESG investing that is investing that puts an emphasis on the environmental social and governance characteristics of companies continues to be popular investors may want to avoid ESG related risks or to feel good about their portfolios which are legitimate motivations but how much good is ESG investing actually doing I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at PWL Capital and I'm going to tell you why a common approach to ESG investing is probably less impactful than you might think investors do care a lot about ESG considerations in March 2,016 Morningstar first published its sustainability ratings for mutual funds they ranked more than 20,000 funds on a percentile basis and assigned globes to denote how sustainable a funds holdings were the worst 10% of funds got a one globe rating while the best 10% got five globes before these ratings it was not easy for an investor to assess how sustainable a mutual fund was when Morningstar deployed the globe ratings the highest rated funds received a substantial uptick in inflows while the lowest rated funds saw outflows this effect is studied in the 2,019 paper in the journal of finance do investors value sustainability a natural experiment examining ranking and fun flows the authors note that while more detailed percentile rank and sustainability scored data were made available investors only responded to the globe ratings suggesting that investors want a simple way to assess sustainability and that they ignore more detailed information even when it's available the authors additionally find that people believe globe ratings are positively related to expected future performance and negatively related to expected riskiness and that people considering environmental or social factors will allocate more to five globe funds no surprise this suggests that globe ratings as a symbol of sustainability affect investors risk and return expectations and lead them to make choices based on non pecunary reasons like feeling good about their portfolio the same study finds that in their sample there's no evidence of higher returns for sustainability funds similar findings across many studies suggest empirically that there has not been a statistically significant difference in performance between ESG and conventional funds in theory ESG funds should have lower expected returns due to investors non pecunary preference to hold them and due to their ability to hedge ESG related risks if investors are hoping for market beating returns from their ESG investments they're likely to be disappointed but that's not the point I wanna focus on the point I wanna focus on is the idea that being an ESG investor is doing some good the main idea with sustainable investing is that avoiding the least sustainable companies will increase their cost of capital pushing them to be greener evidence on the ability of ESG investing to affect the cost of capital is mixed the 2022 paper The Impact of Impact Investing demonstrates that the impact from divestiture on firms cost of capital is too small to impact corporate investment decisions however other research does suggest that sustainability focused investors have been successful at raising the cost of capital for firms with negative environmental impact to the extent that this is true the result may be counterintuitively counterproductive the 2023 paper Counterproductive Sustainable Investing the Impact Elasticity of brown and Green Firms shows empirically that reducing the cost of capital for firms that are already green meaning firms with positive environmental impact leads to at best small improvements in their environmental impact on the other hand increasing the cost of capital for brown firms firms with negative environmental impact leads to large negative changes in impact this means that sustainable investing that directs capital away from brown firms and toward green firms makes brown firms more brown without making green firms more green the reason is simple green firms can't really get much greener and when brown firms are backed into a corner by an increased cost of capital they lean further into their existing high pollution production or they may be even cut corners on pollution mitigation these findings suggest that sustainable investing aimed at increasing the cost of capital of firms with negative environmental impact may actually be as the title suggests counter productive one argument in favor of ESG investing separate from its ability to make the world a better place is that owning green assets act as a hedge against adverse news about the climate and increasing environmental concerns as documented in the 2022 paper Dissecting Green Returns this explains the outperformance of green stocks from November 2,012 through December 2020 the implication is that the historical performance of green stocks are largely supported by an unanticipated increase in environmental concerns over that period on expectation going forward barring another unexpected increase in climate concerns green stocks are expected to underperform brown stocks this is what you would expect from any hedge asset the other reason that investors may want an E s as described in the 2020 paper E s G as waving banners and as pulling plows they want to wave a banner they want the expressive and emotional benefits of staying true to their values but they don't want to sacrifice investment returns the author argues that ESG investors who do no more than for example exclude stocks of fossil fuel producers from their portfolios are banner minded ESG investors who do no actual good for others plow minded ESG investors are willing to make investments expected to earn below market returns in order to do good for example investing in community bonds with below market expected returns and therefore a greater social impact is what the author would call plow minded investing the author argues that the ESG movement has lost its way with investors being less focused on using their capital to do good and more focused on looking and feeling like they're doing good none of this means that thinking about ESG is a bad idea but as explained in the 2022 paper the end of ESG ESG is both extremely important and it's nothing special it's extremely important in the sense that it affects a company's long term shareholder value and its impact on society but it's nothing special in the sense that it's only one of many intangible assets that affect financial and social value ESG investing seems like a good idea but it's usefulness depends on your objectives the ability of ESG investing to have real impact on how companies behave through the cost of capital channel is somewhere between limited and counter productive owning greener assets might provide a hedge against increasing climate concerns but this does come at the cost of lower expected returns at least in theory many ESG minded investors want to feel good and carry the ESG banner which is fine but as Blackrock's former CEO of sustainable investing has explained this could be like selling wheat grass to a cancer patient there is limited evidence to support its efficacy and it might detract attention and capital from more effective activities this doesn't mean that nobody should be an ESG investor hedging ESG risks and feeling good about your portfolio are valid reasons to consider the ESG characteristics of the companies that you own when it comes to making the world a better place I don't have the solution but ESG investing probably isn't it thanks for watching I'm Ben Felix Portfolio Manager at PWL Capital if you're in Canada and want to learn more about how PWL approaches portfolio management and financial planning including how we handle ESG considerations you can book a meeting with us below

## 45. Dollar Cost Averaging vs. Lump Sum Investing
**Channel:** Ben Felix | **Views:** 300K | **Date:** 2 years ago | **Duration:** 5:13 | **ID:** KwR3nxojS0g
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=KwR3nxojS0g

### Transcript:
if you're sitting on a lump sum of cash that you plan to invest you could invest the lump sum in a risk appropriate portfolio immediately you could dollar cost average over a period of time meaning systematically investing equal parts into a risk appropriate portfolio over a set period or you could sit on your cash until what feels like a good time to invest this is a decision that I know torments people when they have cash available cash feels safe and the stock market feels scary there's always a reason whether it's market valuations world events or economic news that makes the current moment in time feel like the scariest time ever to invest I'm Ben Felix Portfolio Manager at PWL Capital and I'm going to tell you how to decide between dollar cost averaging and lump sum investing I wanna be clear before starting that I'm assuming that you have a lump sum to invest if you're saving regular amounts from your paycheck there is no decision to make. You are dollar cost averaging by necessity if you have a lump sum available you need to decide how to enter the market dollar cost averaging feels smart the reason often given for its apparent effectiveness is that when stock prices are high you're buying fewer shares and when prices are low you're buying more shares while mathematically correct dollar cost averaging has been proven objectively sub optimal many times the strongest argument in favor of dollar cost averaging is behavioral behavioral economist Meir Statman offers a framework in a 1995 paper to explain why dollar cost averaging remains popular despite being sub optimal in short dollar cost averaging can help with minimizing regret avoiding investing a lump sum right before a crash by instead making lots of smaller investments despite the behavioral appeal I think it's useful to understand how sub optimal dollar cost averaging is relative to lump sum investing this doesn't mean that data are more important than investor behavior but I think it is important to make decisions from a perspective of understanding rather than a perspective of fear in a 2020 analysis we compared lumpsome investing to dollar cost averaging in six stock markets while the cash was being deployed in our model it earned the rate of return of one month US Treasury bills across the full sample investing a lump sum beat dollar cost averaging about 65% of the time the approximate annualized cost of dollar cost averaging was about 0.38% over 10 years much more than the fee on most index funds whether regret minimization is worth that estimated cost will be a personal decision to try to address common concerns that come up when people have a lump summit to invest we also looked at the performance of dollar cost averaging in a few specific cases in the worst 10% of outcomes for lump sum investments dollar cost averaging does have a small advantage on average but it still trails lump sums more than 50% of the time this is surprising given that we were looking at the worst periods for lump sums with perfect hindsight even if you knew for certain it was a bad time to invest which of course nobody can predict dollar cost averaging is not a silver bullet to improve the outcome if the decision to invest is being made immediately after a 20% or greater drop in the market a time when behavioral biases are likely heightened lump sums continue to be advantageous on average finally we compared investing a lump sum to dollar cost averaging when US stock valuations were in the 95th percentile of expensiveness and found again that lump sum continues to dominate these findings are far from revolutionary a long list of academic papers going back to the 1970s have suggested that dollar cost averaging is sub optimal in a rational decision making framework even if dollar cost averaging is deemed useful from a behavioral perspective I have to question whether the portfolio being deployed in that case is just too risky for the investor a 2016 paper in the Journal of Wealth Management demonstrates theoretically numerically and empirically that dollar cost averaging is approximately equivalent to an asset allocation where only 50 to 65% of the portfolio is invested in risky assets and the rest is invested in riskless assets but that dollar cost averaging is sub optimal compared with a portfolio with a constant 50 to 65% risky allocation the authors conclude that dollar cost averaging should be recommended with care since risk averse investors could be better off investing a lump sum in a more conservative asset allocation instead of dollar cost averaging into a more aggressive one rather than investing a lump sum or dollar cost averaging many investors opt to wait for a drop in the level of market prices before investing they opt to buy the dip in a separate analysis we looked at waiting for a 10 or 20% market decline to invest a lump sum and found that buying the dip underperforms on average and in most ten year periods in our sample waiting for a 20% drop gives up more return and underperformed more frequently than waiting for a 10% drop this even holds true when the analysis is constrained to starting months that are all time highs in the market if you have a lump sum to invest it is likely optimal to invest it in a risk appropriate portfolio as soon as possible there is a strong behavioral argument for dollar cost averaging though if you're so worried about regretting the decision to invest a lump sum that you deem dollar cost averaging necessary it might be a sign to reconsider your asset allocation thanks for watching I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at P W L Capital if you enjoyed this video please share it with someone who is struggling to decide how to invest a lump sum of cash

## 46. High Income Investments: A Warning
**Channel:** Ben Felix | **Views:** 41K | **Date:** 2 years ago | **Duration:** 4:01 | **ID:** 2Q7-SkDEPB8
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=2Q7-SkDEPB8

### Transcript:
distribution yields are a seemingly easy to understand attribute of investments making them a focal point for many investors the salience of yield is no secret to financial product manufacturers resulting in products being marketed based on their high distribution yields the part that's often ignored though is that distribution yields and investment returns are not necessarily the same thing many investments with high distribution yields have low expected returns when total returns are considered not to mention the potential for tax inefficiency I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at PWL Capital and I'm going to tell you why investments with high distribution yields can be misleading investment products including mutual funds ETFs and structured products are often marketed to investors based on their yields with no regard for or indication of their expected returns this is problematic because distribution yields do not necessarily have any relationship to expected returns and empirically many investments with high distribution yields have performed poorly for investors The Ontario Securities Commission recently published an issue oriented review with their observation that some funds have been marketed as having yields as high as 15% I'm looking at you covered call ETFs which is a problem not because it's factually inaccurate but because it is emphasized over other aspects of the funds expected return profile when investors see a 15% yield they imagine a 15% expected return but these two things are not the same in the case of covered call funds most of the yield is coming from option premiums but writing options on the funds holdings gives a right to a third party to purchase the shares in the portfolio if the stock rises above a certain price which means giving up the upside of the stock the result is a lower expected return than the underlying stock portfolio despite a high distribution yield it's easy to see that the large yields targeted by covered call funds have not been related to their realized total returns take HMAX the Hamilton Canadian Financials Yield Maximizer ETF it targets a 13% yield but its total return since inception in January 2023 has been negative 4.69% under performing Canadian financials or a more extreme example the yield Max Tesla Option Income Strategy ETF has a distribution rate of 60% but a total return dramatically below the underlying Tesla stock since inception for a fund with more history the BMO covered call Canadian Banks ETF has a 10 year return trailing the underlying holdings despite having a much higher distribution yield a similar observation can be made in structured products for high headline rates of return which are like the best case scenario for the product and resemble a yield are unrelated to the product expected returns and realized returns but they're used to attract investors the demand for structured products is largely driven by their yields with little regard for their expected total returns and less sophisticated households are targeted by banks issuing products with high yields similar to covered calls the result is that investors looking for yield end up unwittingly investing in assets with high costs and low expected returns juicy yields are also used in regular equity mutual funds to attract investors some mutual funds intentionally buy stocks before dividend payments artificially driving up the funds yield again investors love it funds with juiced up dividend yields see larger inflows and seem to be most attractive to unsophisticated investors chasing the juicy yield is expensive for the funds' investors due to turnover and increased taxes estimated at 0.57% to 1.52% in added costs per year distribution yields are not investment returns they're not sufficient to assess expected investment returns and in the way that they're used to market financial products often to unsophisticated investors they are often misleading total returns are what matter to investors including to investors who needed income from their portfolio thanks for watching I'm Ben Felix Portfolio Manager at PWL Capital if you enjoyed this video please share it with the people in your life who obsess over the distribution yield of their investments

## 47. The Cost of Investment Hubris
**Channel:** Ben Felix | **Views:** 87K | **Date:** 2 years ago | **Duration:** 4:09 | **ID:** MJaqZRd1bTc
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=MJaqZRd1bTc

### Transcript:
Warren Buffett is famously said that diversification is a protection against ignorance and that it makes very little sense for anyone that knows what they're doing this quote is often used as an argument against diversified investment strategies like low cost index funds but the thing is Warren Buffett was probably not talking about you when he referred to people who know what they're doing I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at PWL Capital and I'm going to tell you why investment hubris can cost you in the long run this Buffett quote is a common response when I mention that index funds are sensible investments for most people any investor engaging in stock selection or market timing presumably thinks that they do know what they're doing otherwise they would be buying and holding a low cost portfolio of index funds but if you think that you're included Buffett's group of people who know what they're doing I would like to offer up another Buffett quote and some supporting research in his 2016 letter to shareholders Buffett explains that while there are of course some skilled individuals who are highly likely to outperform the S&P over long stretches he is identified early on only 10 or so professionals that he expected would accomplish this feat Buffett does acknowledge that there may be hundreds perhaps thousands of people out there who can accomplish this feat but even still we're talking about a tiny portion of the population early on is an important qualifier in Buffett's sentence it's easy to identify successful investors after the fact but difficult to know whether their success is repeatable in the future this is important because attempting to follow in the footsteps of a lucky investor is a fool's errant for what I hope are obvious reasons respectfully I think it takes a certain amount of hubris to include yourself in the small group of investors the group that Buffett is referring to that may be able to beat the market in the long run by selecting securities and timing the market the cost of that hubris has been quantified in a recent paper titled The Cost of Investment Hubris the author analytically estimates the cost of stock selection and market timing for investors who believe they possess such skills but actually don't they find that uninformed market timing reduces risk adjusted returns by about 1.3% per year and uninformed stock selection costs around 0.8% per year due to under diversification even if as many as 20 stocks are included in the portfolio those estimates do not include trading costs and taxes which can be material especially considering that overconfident investors tend to trade more resulting in higher costs and taxes and lower net returns even for professional fund managers those with higher active share a metric that gauges how different a manager is from their benchmark index and could be used as a proxy for conviction or confidence in their ability to trade successfully tend to deliver worse performance and more risk despite being quoted in support of concentrated portfolios Buffett has been a strong proponent of index funds his regular recommendation to people asking for investing advice is a low cost S&P 500 index fund which is advice he says most people of modest means do tend to follow well mega rich individuals who may feel they deserve something extra in investment advice due to their wealth will tend to seek out more elaborate investment solutions unfortunately many of the supposed benefits of more elaborate asset classes recommended to wealthy investors including private equity real estate and hedge funds are probably more fairy tale than reality but as Buffett explains it would be career suicide for an investment consultant to continuously tell people to buy index funds investing can be and I would argue should be pretty simple owning a low cost diversified portfolio when sticking with a strategy through good times and bad is a recipe for success I like to say somewhat tongue in cheek but also somewhat seriously that investing has been solved despite knowing what the solution looks like low cost index funds for most people lots of people believe that they are one of the few who can outperform by picking stocks timing the market identifying a successful manager before the fact or investing in exotic investment strategies targeted at wealthy investors the reality is that most people will not outperform through this means and the cost of their investment hubris is likely to be substantial thanks for watching I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at PWL Capital if you enjoyed this video please share it with someone who has investment hubris

## 48. The Performance Gap
**Channel:** Ben Felix | **Views:** 58K | **Date:** 2 years ago | **Duration:** 3:32 | **ID:** IVJkTspjDUo
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=IVJkTspjDUo

### Transcript:
everybody wants their investments to outperform the market but most investors underperform for a simple reason they chase past performance investors get into investments after they have done well and out after they've done poorly resulting in a sizable gap between investor returns and investment returns I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at PWL Capital and I'm going to tell you why most investors underperform the market and how you can do better performance chasing is a persistent feature of financial markets it explains aggregate flows to mutual funds and the flows across funds and holds for both actively managed and index funds the resulting performance gap is between 1.5 and 2% depending on the source and it tends to be much larger for more volatile specialized and speculative funds for example the gap was 5.3% for the NASDAQ from 1973 to 2002 4.38% for sector funds in the decade ending December 2022 and for an extreme example investors in Cathie Wood's ARKK fund earn double digit negative dollar weighted returns on average while the fund delivered positive time weighted returns from inception to May 2023 more generally the return gap tends to be much larger for growth oriented funds than for value oriented funds fees get a lot of attention and don't get me wrong fees are very important but these data suggest that investors are losing more on average and in some cases much more per year to in opportune timing than they would pay to own a high fee actively managed mutual fund the data on flows and the impact of flows on performance should not be too surprising given the relationship between expected returns and subject of return expectations investors tend to have high return expectations when model based expected returns are low and valuations are high and low subjective return expectations when model based expected returns are high and valuations are low in other words investors extrapolate recent returns into the future so they'll be more likely to believe that a fund with strong recent returns and correspondingly holdings with likely high valuations will also have strong future returns and that a fund that has done poorly will continue to do so this will tend to be an error diversified funds will tend to have persistent exposure to common factors like market beta size and value which will explain most of the funds returns when a fund's factor exposures have a period of poor performance it drags down the fund's return driving investors away and pushes down the valuation of the factors leading to higher expected returns for the fund it works in the other direction too a fund with strong past performance which probably looks enticing to investors likely loads on factors that have recently done well and correspondingly have higher valuations and lower expected returns timing errors occur both on the upside that is buying after recent positive past performance and on the downside selling after a decline but selling after a decline seems to contribute more to the overall performance gap at least in some studies I think these data highlight the importance of having a portfolio and investment strategy more generally that you can stick with you need to be able to live with your portfolios volatility and in particular downside volatility and to avoid getting distracted by attention grabbing recent returns of other investments beating the market is hard but beating the average dollar invested in the market is relatively easy if you can avoid making the all too common mistake of performance chasing thanks for watching I'm Ben Felix Portfolio Manager at PWL Capital if you enjoyed this video please share it with your return chasing friends

## 49. Why NOT Investing Is (Probably) Going To Cost You
**Channel:** Ben Felix | **Views:** 81K | **Date:** 2 years ago | **Duration:** 7:08 | **ID:** 1qjSfyVPwLQ
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=1qjSfyVPwLQ

### Transcript:
if worrying about the next market crash is affecting your investment decisions you might be making an expensive mistake people spend so much time worrying about the next market downturn that they miss out on the positive expected returns available in risky assets I'm Ben Felix portfolio manager at PWL Capital and I'm going to tell you why you should expect pessimism to cost you in the long run stocks have been an incredible wealth generating tool around the world for hundreds of years but many investors hold pessimistic beliefs about the stock market and those with more pessimistic beliefs invest less in stocks leaving potential wealth on the table as far back as we have data the optimists people who have invested in stocks have typically triumphed. It is true that some stock markets have delivered total losses and some markets have delivered really low stock returns but global stock returns have been incredible for at least the last 123 years delivering an annualized 5% real return measured in US dollars from 1900 through 2022 data for the Netherlands and the UK back to 1629 and data back to 1372 for one French company suggest that a long term real return of at least 5% for stocks has been persistent for hundreds of years to be clear stock markets have been and will continue to be volatile and they're certainly not risk free in the short run or the long run it's possible to lose money in real terms even over a 30 year period especially investing in a single country but even accounting for failed markets and bad outcomes diversified and disciplined long term investors have typically been rewarded despite the evidence investors worry about their investments day to day even if day to day drops won't affect their long term financial plan and they assess the probabilities of a market crash like the 1987 or 1929 crashes to be about an order of magnitude larger than the actual historical frequency of those types of events this matters because pessimistic beliefs affect how people invest subjective crash probabilities are negatively associated with flows and equity mutual funds and the equity share of investors portfolios is influenced by their perception of rare disaster risk market crashes are certainly painful but markets have delivered positive long term returns despite many historical crashes pessimism is influenced at least in part by the media which has an asymmetric effect on crash beliefs articles with negative sentiment increase crash beliefs while those with positive sentiment have little effect additionally bad stock market outcomes receive disproportionately pessimistic coverage in the media and there's a general negativity bias in reporting macroeconomic news when market crashes do inevitably happen investors expectations of stock market returns become more pessimistic despite the evidence that falling stock prices usually indicate higher expected returns rather than lower and that the probability of a large positive return is higher following a crash more generally investors subjective expectations about future returns are inversely related to market expected returns that last point is incredibly important people tend to revise their subjective return expectations their feelings about future stock returns downward when market expected returns are high leading to an underallocation to stocks arguably at the worst possible times the fact that expected returns seem to increase when stock prices fall make stocks a little bit less risky in the long run than they would be if returns were completely random personal experiences also matter a lot to investors expectations about the future even though personal experience should not have anything to do with the objective assessment of expected returns people who have experienced low stock market returns throughout their lives are less willing to take risk allocate less of their wealth to stocks and are more pessimistic about future stock returns there are also asymmetries in the way that people learn from stock market information the formation of beliefs when learning from losses tends to be overly pessimistic overly sensitive to bad outcomes and further away from an objective assessment of expectations compared to the formation of beliefs when learning from gains these errors lead investors to hold more conservative portfolios than a more optimistic investor would to avoid participating in the stock market altogether and to sabotage their long term returns with short term decision making the good news is there are some potential solutions an easy one is to look at your investments less frequently people are myopically loss of verse and we know empirically that seeing your investments less frequently is associated with earning higher returns automating or delegating more of your portfolio management process to reduce how frequently you have to interact with your investments may be helpful for example investors lose less of their returns to inopportune investment timing decisions in asset allocation funds which rebalance automatically than they do in equity funds my hunch is that this is at least partially related to rebalancing after equities drop being difficult for many investors buying stocks when pessimism and expected returns are high and subjective return expectations are low is not always easy automating your investment process is only helpful if you're investing in stocks to begin with which many pessimists are not financial literacy does seem to increase stock market participation so watching videos like this may be useful tuning out the financial media is another easy win the media makes investors more pessimistic without offering them any useful information by the time the media is reporting on something that thing is already reflected in prices media content does not contain new information about fundamental asset values since many of these issues are related to errors in base rates for example not knowing what long term expected returns are or not understanding how expected returns vary over time getting an outside perspective may be useful financial advisors do seem to increase participation in the stock market and boost the share of stocks investors portfolios this makes sense to the extent that financial advisors beliefs are more informed than their clients trust in a financial advisor may also reduce the perceived riskiness of investments and allow risk of verse investors to earn higher expected returns with a financial advisor than they would on their own it's easy to say that people don't need financial advice because everyone should go and buy low cost etfs in their discount brokerage account which is something that I agree with but a lot of people simply won't do that well financial advisors may help people to get invested and get the right amount of stock exposure it's a bit of a mindfield it's important to watch out for high fees and conflict of interest which could easily offset the benefits of financial advice and to be wary of the fact that many financial advisors make the same errors that a typical individual investor would pessimism is expensive the stock market is an incredible tool for long term wealth generation but to benefit from it you have to participate in it consistently myopic loss of version over estimating the probability of crashes having pessimistic subjective return expectations when expected returns are high and conditioning expectations about the future on negative personal experiences can lead to missing out on the returns that the market has to offer looking at your investments less frequently automating or delegating your investment process increasing your financial literacy getting an outside perspective and hiring a skilled financial advisor with reasonable fees and limited conflict of interest are all potential solutions to reducing the long term costs of pessimism thanks for watching I'm Ben Felix Portfolio Manager at PWL Capital if you enjoyed this video please share it with the last person who told you that you need to go to cash because the market is about to crash

## 50. Is an 8% Safe Withdrawal Rate... Safe?
**Channel:** Ben Felix | **Views:** 68K | **Date:** 2 years ago | **Duration:** 2:55 | **ID:** Hhg6dB2UcAs
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=Hhg6dB2UcAs

### Transcript:
Dave Ramsey recently claimed that an 8% withdrawal rate from an investment portfolio is safe for retirees safe withdrawal rates estimate the percentage of a portfolio that can be spent in the first year and then adjusted for inflation thereafter without a material risk of running out of money Dave's logic is that good mutual funds return around 12% per year and inflation has averaged around 4% for the last 80 years this leaves 8% to be spent by the retiree unfortunately the logic is flawed and retirement spending math does not work this way good mutual funds are hard to find before the fact so the idea that you can easily pick a market beating fund is a flimsy Assumption the best performing funds historically do not tend to go on to be the best performing funds in the future a similar comment can be made about country returns Dave references the performance of the S&P 500 as being a little below 12% but again the phenomenal historical performance of the S&P 500 does not mean that future returns for US stocks will be similarly high it would be far more sensible at least in my opinion to use return experiences of countries around the world to gain an understanding of possible return scenarios in planning for retirement when you do this the safe withdrawal rate is closer to 3% or even a little lower even if we could find a fund that does return 12% on average it would not sustain an 8% withdrawal rate for the simple reason that stock returns are not constant that 12% average return will consist of lots of big ups and downs and inflation also goes through higher and lower periods constantly spending an inflation adjusted 8% of the initial portfolio during consecutive down years or having to keep up with years of higher inflation without offsetting higher returns can deplete a portfolio quickly let's take a mutual fund that has returned about 12% per year since 1935 The American Funds Investment Company of America it returned 11.73% annualized from 1934 through October 2023 which is incredible now as a side note despite its incredible long term track record this fund has trailed the US market for more than 20 years as of today you can't buy past performance but anyway using this fund's historical performance to test an 8% withdrawal in the first year followed by annual inflation adjustments so a constant real $80,000 in spending on a million dollar portfolio over a 30 year period yields a success rate of a little more than 50% this means that in around 50% of simulated trials you ran out of money before the end of the 30 year withdrawal period I don't know about you but I would not call that safe at a 5% failure rate the safe withdrawal rate for this fund which we know has a great historical track record would be about 4.6% come on Dave be better
